Nothing was deleted and nothing was pruned. Every `## ` heading below is the heading it carried in the hot file, and the hot file carries a stub under each one.**

**LEFT IN PLACE, DELIBERATELY: `## Rolling volume summary` (the standing one-paragraph-per-volume summary the index says to read instead of the archives), `## Volumes 5–16`, `## Volume 6 - The Real Realm`, `## Volume 6 closed - audited`, the Volume 1 to Volume 5 blocks, and `## VOLUME 14 BATCH 0001` onward.**

**36 `## ` sections moved.** Read a section by its heading.

## Volume 7 planned — *Stronger Things*, ch 361–420

**`outline/volume-07.md` was written by the Volume 7 planning phase. No Volume 7 chapter exists. This block is the
plan, not a summary of prose, and it is here so that the Volume 7 drafting phases and the Volume 7 close have a
single place to walk against.**

### The shape of it

Sixty chapters, day 849 to day 1,090, four batches of 14 / 15 / 15 / 16, six Nascent Soul stages, six bracketed layer
lines. Goal fixed by `outline/series.md` §9 and not re-planned: *In this volume, Ning Yan must survive being the
weakest person in every room she walks into, or she learns that strength was never what she was taken for.*

### The four batches

| Batch | Chapters | Days | Pressure | Midpoint | Climax | Irreversible step |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0001 | 361–374 (14) | 849–899 | **rooms bigger than she is** | ch 368 — the yard of nine men where she is the smallest thing in it | ch 371 — Nascent Soul 2, and thirteen days of a shed with a fire in it that does not come up | the first stage taken in the open with people watching (ch 371) |
| 0002 | 375–389 (15) | 900–950 | **what she is worth in a room** | ch 383 — Nascent Soul 3, and a false line can no longer be entered | ch 388 — she says a name out loud and asks | **she asks, and cannot un-ask** |
| 0003 | 390–404 (15) | 951–1,020 | **the reach on purpose** | ch 394 — Nascent Soul 4, and it does not go back | ch 401–402 — the third frame scene and the break | **the answer goes up, and she can never refuse a reading again** |
| 0004 | 405–420 (16) | 1,021–1,090 | **what it costs to be right** | ch 412 — Nascent Soul 6, and the bare board becomes the rest of the run | ch 417 the refusal · ch 419 the lift and the Hand refused · ch 420 Nascent Soul 7 and the column read out loud | **she declines to know what the fine hand says about her, forever, on purpose** |

### The six stages, and the six costs, each named once

- **Nascent Soul 2 · ch 371 · day 886 · the third of a month · 41 days after NS 1** — **the furnace will not light
  itself and she cannot light it alone.**
- **Nascent Soul 3 · ch 383 · day 924 · the eleventh · 38 days** — **her hand stops being able to lie.**
- **Nascent Soul 4 · ch 394 · day 960 · the seventeenth · 36 days** — **a thing she reaches cannot be put back where
  it was.**
- **Nascent Soul 5 · ch 404 · day 999 · the twenty-sixth · 39 days** — **she cannot be given anything.**
- **Nascent Soul 6 · ch 412 · day 1,037 · the fourth · 38 days** — **she cannot be paid.**
- **Nascent Soul 7 · ch 420 · day 1,076 · the thirteenth · 39 days** — **the reach will not go out of a room she has
  not been asked into.**

### The three new people, and the one that stays unnamed

**Rao** keeps the columns on the third floor, sixteen years, and is the midpoint. **Dai** can read the fine hand and
offers once and is refused once. **Geng** runs the lifting floor, is strong and correct, and his floor is a block
short for a week. **The keeper with the paste pot stays unnamed**, as in Volume 6, and carries the heaviest scene.

### The structural answer, six mouths

ch 365 (a carter) · ch 379 (a gate man) · ch 391 (Rao) · ch 398 (a woman who drives clay) · ch 409 (a copying man
below Rao) · ch 415 (Geng). **Four or five sentences each, never as a speech. The tenth part is not given and nothing
proves the crews wrong.**

### The hand-off to Volume 8

**Not one word of the Order is on Volume 7's page.** The question Volume 7 opens onto it: *the last strip went east
and did not come back, she has never been east of here, and something in a country that big has been sending for
her by name since before she knew she was being read — what happens to the first person who comes for the reader.*

**Figures:** 95,110 words of body prose stand on the page for Volume 6 (24,529 · 23,975 · 25,056 · 21,550 by batch),
360 `#` headings, zero collisions. **Volume 7 contributes zero words and zero headings until it is drafted.**

## Volume 7 Batch 0001 — *Stronger Things*, ch 361–374, day 849–899 — WRITTEN AND REVIEWED AND REPAIRED

**Rooms bigger than she is.** 27,114 words of body prose over fourteen chapters, average ~1,937, all fourteen inside the 1,800–2,700 guide. One bracketed layer
line, at ch 371, which is bracket 1 of Volume 7's six in sixty. Full record in `state/continuity.md` under
***Established facts, Volume 7 Batch 0001 (ch 361–374) — ROOMS BIGGER THAN SHE IS*** and, for the six findings and what
was done about them, under ***Volume 7 Batch 0001 — REVIEW REPAIR***.

| Ch | Day | Month-day | Title | Summary |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 361 | **849** | the 26th | The Beginning Of A Habit | The morning after ch 360, at the gate, with a strip folded in two inside her coat that came down on the twenty-fifth. **The bar takes two hands, so the gate now works on a short iron borrowed out of the gate house at dawn and returned before she leaves, and the reach has been a fifth since ch 353 because of it.** She walks three miles to a board that has been bare for about a month and notices she has been doing it four days running with nothing to look for, and does not stop. **The turn: nothing has come for her and nothing is going to**, and the only thing that comes instead is a strip in a hand she cannot read. Wants somebody to come; says out loud on the walk west that she would have gone back if they had. Yuan silent. |
| 362 | 850–851 | the 27th–28th | The Third Sum | **A month is thirty coppers.** She does the whole of it standing up in a gateway on the twenty-seventh: bread twenty, a third of a sack three, seven over. A third of a sack is three of the seven and four coppers is two days of bread. The store is five miles west and its woman has never asked her anything. She asks the man who keeps the list whether the board is the only place work comes from and is told no, and then told that a yard does not put a line on a board for a woman it does not have on its books, and that a book she is on is a book somebody reads. **Yuan, present tense, buys nothing: *Thirty days. You have counted them three times and you counted the paper wrong on one of them.*** She counted the third sum wrong because she left out the paper, which is not money and never was. |
| 363 | 851 | the 28th | Eleven Years Of Copying Down | The copying floor and the counter, **the man with the brush appearing once in this batch**. She asks him to read the four lines in the middle of her strip; he cannot read that hand and never has and has looked for eleven years. **A thing in a hand she cannot read gets written down by somebody and goes up a stair and comes down a road and gets set against another thing, and the answer to that does not come down.** The counter cannot read it either and will not send it back down in his hand. He tells her what the end of a month is: everything that comes into the room in thirty days goes up the mill road at the sixth hour with a cord round it, shutting or not, **including the refusal she gave a month ago in front of nine men.** She spent her question a month and got the same answer for the third time. |
| 364 | 852–853 | the 29th–30th | Read Out Of The Room | **The mechanism, shown before it is named.** At the works gateway on the twenty-ninth a man in a yard's coat is read one line out loud, with the weight in it, and says the figures back from memory and carries a slip east. **The reading has left the room and gone to a person.** Then on the thirtieth the same man is read all eleven lines at once and takes four slips — **a reading can make a crowd.** She works out on the walk west why she had not seen it in six months: every reading that had ever touched her had a person inside it, and this one had none. Yuan, present tense, three sentences, buys nothing: *Then stop asking the question and watch the thing.* She decides to be standing in that yard at the sixth hour on the first of a month, eleven days' walk out of the ninth hour, with a week's bread. |
| 365 | 853–855 | the 30th, the 1st, the 2nd | Where The Cart Goes | **The first thing that has ever gone east on that ground.** At ten minutes past the sixth hour on the first, the tray is corded and the mill cart goes up the mill road past the elms; and a strip about as wide as a hand, folded in two with a stone on it, goes on the tail-board of the second cart and out of the yard east. She watches it until it is a shape on the flat. The counter's boy is sure it goes up the mill road. An east carter with a chain over the cart bed tells her the road is four miles to the turn and five more and then the long road — **and mouth one of Volume 7's six: he has been turned back at the end of it four hundred times, turning back is the ordinary end of the road, turning back is what people pay for, they pay for the going out and they pay for the stopping, and there is no part in the middle.** Asked whether it comes back: no, and it will be on the cart that takes the empties up, and there is a different man on that one. |
| 366 | 856–864 | the 3rd–11th | Two Different Facts | The east cart comes back in **empty** behind a load of timber, and she stands at the gate post looking at a bare tail-board. **The mechanism in four lines: a strip that goes up the mill road comes back on the ninth with the empty trays, and a strip that goes east does not, because that cart does not go east.** Four ordinary days at a fifth of a reach. On the ninth the empty-tray cart comes down with four nested trays, a rope and a stone, **and nothing else at all**, and she stands nine feet off and watches it go past the elms. The plain part about one person: she is eighteen and cannot read the hand, and the answer came back as less and she wants to walk nine miles east and there is not one thing she could do at either end. Pei is right about the drain going shallower and says so at the eighth hour on the eleventh. **The season turns on the eleventh.** |
| 367 | 864 | the 11th | It Comes Back Full | **The board comes back full — eleven lines, and not one of them says a length.** Every line is dressed stone for a yard east, priced by the ton. She can take none, and she works out standing in the gateway why a length line is not hers either: it goes to whoever is at the wall when a man's hand is on it, and there are nine men in that yard. Then the listing man reads the board out loud to a man from a yard and hands him four slips, and she watches a reading make a crowd. She asks whether he will read it for money. **The reading is not a trade, is not on anybody's books and is not paid, and a board has one man who reads it.** He tells her he has known she could read a column since the day she stood in that gateway and asked to be put in an account, and that knowing it is not work. She is angry at him for two minutes and he is right about all of it, and she writes down with the flat of her second finger the whole of what she can do: a fifth of a reach, cutting by length, reading — **and there is nothing she can put against the third one.** |
| 368 | 867–869 | the 14th–16th | **The Ninth Man** — the batch midpoint | **The yard at the turn four miles east: brick and timber, a covered bay, a bar against the end post, nine men on a board where four will do.** She is the smallest thing in the bay and a man with a rule in his hand takes her head to the boots in about two seconds, most of it the right arm. **He asks her for a thing she can do and then a thing she cannot, and he is right about both.** The length is a hedge, four hundred yards at the east end, and it is on his board and is not hers — no name of hers goes anywhere in that yard — and she gets the foot of it up wrong and does the last hundred yards again in the dark. Paid in a cup of something hot from a woman who has never asked her name. The sill is two hundred and something of dressed stone, eleven pieces to the tonne, and **he had worked out which of his nine she was at the second hour yesterday and it is the one that is not on the board, and he never intended to ask twice.** She wanted him to make her ask twice and he did not. **The chapter's finding: a yard prices a body and every office she has been entered in has her column in it, and nowhere on nine days of road has her arm on a line of her own.** |
| 369 | 873–875 | the 20th–22nd | Four People Agree Later | The hedge is closed off the board — done, checked, off — and she walked four miles to find out whether a board keeps the thing you did. The man with the rule pushes a barrow-load of her own gate clay back at her: he has four tons coming off that bank every day and a man paid to be on it, and he will not take clay off a woman at a gate. Nine men take no notice of her the second morning, and that is the point: **she has been priced, and it took about two seconds with a rule in a hand.** Then, nine feet off the cart track, the **Hunter**, notebook shut, buys nothing, asks nothing, and she asks him — **what a room on this ground is for** — and he answers in the ordinary voice and at no cost to either of them: **a room is where a thing is put so that four people can agree about it later.** Then: still here, still buying nothing, not going to tell her why, and she is not going to ask. She works it out on the walk home: **she had been the thing in every room she had walked into for six months and had been getting it wrong.** Yuan, two sentences, the arrangement. |
| 370 | 884–885 | the 1st–2nd | The Last Day It Lit Itself | **Merit settles once, on a listed clearing, not on a death** — the hedge, closed and checked and off on the twentieth, done with her hands and off nobody's book. **The share is six days and four hours and he says it out loud twice before she spends it**, because she is going to spend it before she has had a night with it and he would rather she heard it. She spends it on nothing; it goes out of the ground under her and does not come back. She puts her hand on the ring to say goodnight to it, and it is warm. **Then the action card: the second of a month, a bank a mile and a quarter east of the mill, nine men on a face about sixty feet long.** A bar comes out of the face with four pieces of the heavy stone on it and comes down the low side, and she is on the low side filling a barrow. About a ton. **She lifts — the thing she learned at a bank nine days west in three words — and it comes up out of the ground under her ribs and goes under what is on top of her, and the bar stops about a finger's width** and two men take the rest. She stands up whole, and puts her hands out in front of her, **and they are cold.** They had been warm for four months. |
| 371 | **886** | the 3rd | **NASCENT SOUL 2** — What Two Men Saw From The Bank | The fire does not come up at the fourth hour and will not come up with clay, with ash or with her hands flat on the brick. At the seventh hour, out on the flat past the last hedge, **on the high side of a bank with nine men on it and two of them nine feet off**, the stage comes out of the ground she is standing on. [Ning Yan, past the line, servant-born, under no ceiling any county holds. Nascent Soul, stage two, is held.] **Forty-one days after Nascent Soul 1, the longest interval in the volume**, and the chapter says why: the board was bare, the season had not turned, and a stage needs material she had no way to pay for. Nothing goes out of her body and the bill comes four hours later in the shed: she tries it nine times over three hours and it comes up a finger's width and stops every time. **Cost, named once: the furnace will not light itself and she cannot light it alone. A stage cannot be built by one pair of hands.** And the part that cannot be put back: **two men looked at her, and they will say it to the next man who stands on that high side, and there is no book inside nine days of road that can be un-corded.** This batch's irreversible step. |
| 372 | 887–891 | the 4th–8th | The Ring Stays Cold | Thirteen days begin. The fire does not come up on any of the first four, and by the eighth she has stopped expecting it, which is worse, because a thing you expect and do not get is a thing you get up for. **The whole of a day in eleven hours of walking and four hours of standing**: a fifth of a reach, one copper, the store five miles west, the works three miles east with a full board, a length at the yard three days in seven, and one mile and a quarter of flat she does not go out onto. **Half a box of clay is four days of the ring and there is no ring**, so the clay is a barrow against a wall. **Her hands are cold, and the flat stone by the door is cold, and for the first time in four years the two are the same temperature and it tells her nothing.** Four things she could have done about it and does not do — the counter, the yard, her own book off the stone under the keeper's ladder, and asking. The chapter says the reason out loud on the fifth: a thing that comes into a room gets put in a room so that four people can agree about it later, and she cannot read the hand. |
| 373 | 892–893 | the 9th–10th | Two Lines Shorter | She carries half a box of her own gate clay to the yard in a barrow and is told no, correctly: he has four tons coming off that bank and a man paid to be on it. Then, at the second hour on the tenth, **the man with the brush has been waiting since the sixth to give her something before she spends her question.** The cross-reference: her column set against the other book once a month, and this month the column in the fine hand has **two lines at the foot of it where it had four for four months.** He cannot read the hand and refuses to guess at what two means. **She works it out at the bottom of that stair: something went east on the first at a quarter past the sixth hour and something came back, and it came back the other way — down the mill road on the ordinary monthly copy — with two lines off it.** It did not come back on the cart. She does not ask her question. She asks instead whether he will set her column against it again next month, and he says that is the cross-reference and it is not done for her. **She is a person two offices vouch for and she has never once been able to read what she has been vouched for.** |
| 374 | 897–899 | the 14th–16th | The Seventh Person | **Thirteen days of a ring that comes up about a finger's width and stops, counted twice with the flat of her second finger.** The whole of what she did with them: **thirteen coppers in and one trip to the store at eight, which leaves five**, thirty-nine miles each way, a length six days at the yard and a cup of something hot six days, six days at a full board, four barrow-loads of clay against the outside wall, one trip up the mill road, and nothing spent. **Pei, at the eighth hour, is right about the drain going from four feet to three and a half and says so, and it is the one thing a man at a gate is allowed to say.** The plain part about one person, and the chapter's line: **A stage cannot be built by one pair of hands, and it took four months of that fire coming up on its own to teach her so.** She works out six people she could name and every one of them somebody she would not ask — the rule man, the counter, the listing man, the counter on the mill road, the man with the brush, the gate man, the carter. **And the seventh: the man with the paste pot at a post on the mill road, who cannot read her book and would not tell anybody, and the counting house is two miles short of his own ladder on a road she walks four or five times a month.** She says the thing out loud at the second hour — that she has to put a person in that room and say it and mean it — and then says she has not said it to anybody, and does not say it to a man in a gateway, or a man with a bench, or the layer. |

## Volume 7 Batch 0002 — *Stronger Things*, ch 375–389, day 900–950 — WRITTEN

**What she is worth in a room.** 28,296 words of body prose over fifteen chapters, average ~1,886 — *(corrected at the Batch 0004 review; the 27,966 / ~1,864 this line carried did not reproduce under any counting method)* — fourteen of the fifteen inside the 1,800–2,700 guide and ch 385 under it at 1,792, none under the 1,300 floor. One bracketed layer
line, at ch 383, which is bracket 2 of Volume 7's six in sixty. Full record in `state/continuity.md` under
***Established facts, Volume 7 Batch 0002 (ch 375–389) — WHAT SHE IS WORTH IN A ROOM*** and, for the ten fault
categories found by measurement and cut before saving, under ***Construction budget — Volume 7 Batch 0002 (ch
375–389)***.

| Ch | Day | Month-day | Title | Summary |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 375 | **900** | the **17th** | What A Keeper Has To Fetch | **The morning after ch 374, and the ring is at a finger's width for the fourteenth day running.** Five coppers in the fold and no bread since the twelfth, and eight coppers again on the twentieth, and she is not hungry and she is light in the head for three days out of seven. **The arithmetic of her whole money life, named once: seven coppers in seven days, eight out of bread, a copper a week short since she came past this mill, and the difference has been eating a stock that is now nothing.** Pei says the thing he has one thing to say and she does not spend it twice. **Then she works out the engine of the next fortnight standing in a gateway: a man who keeps a board has to fetch his own strips, paper comes out of one building on that ground, and he fetches it at the counting house on the first of a month with the cart — fourteen days off.** She is not going to the post. She is going to the counting house. **The plain part: she wanted somebody to be angry with her, and there is nobody on nine days of road she has a right to be angry with.** Yuan, one sentence. |
| 376 | 901–904 | the 18th–21st | Seven In And Eight Out | **Seven in and eight out, done out loud, and the last thing she could buy with money is clay, which does nothing.** She goes without on purpose. **The board at the works has been turned round** so that the eleven lines face the gateway, and the man who keeps the list tells her it was not turned for her and would rather she heard it than worked it out from the cover being up. She tries the one thing the volume forbids her — she stands in the counting house for twenty-five minutes and says nothing — **and the man behind the counter sends her out in one sentence: everything that comes into this room gets put in this room.** She had a question a month in her and does not spend it on a bench or a room. |
| 377 | 905–909 | the 22nd–26th | Loaded And Going East | **The sill line goes east past the turn on the twenty-second and she walks with it, and for the first time in seven months she goes east of the turn.** Out five and a half miles, to a rise, and a long low bank about four miles further on that is not trees, and nothing comes off it and nothing makes a sound. She turns round and walks nine and a half miles back, fills a cut in a horse trough, and takes a cup from the bench of a bay of nine men because the woman in the office end will not hand one to her in the open. **The plain part: the only thing she learned on nine miles of road is that a woman standing on a rise with a bar on her shoulder is a shape on a flat, and that somebody on this ground could have looked up.** The man with the rule in his hand names the ditch line, refuses to put her on it, and tells her the sill takes one of his men on the twenty-second. Yuan, one sentence. |
| 378 | 910–914 | the 27th–30th, then the 1st | **The Hand On The Ground** | **THE SECOND PAIR OF HANDS. IT WORKS ONCE AND THE BURN IS ON A MAN SHE OWED NOTHING TO.** Ten coppers at the thirtieth and eleven on the first. On the first of a month at the sixth hour, in a low building with nine men in it, **a man about fifty-five with a paste pot and a rag and a bundle of strips to fetch gets down off a high stool with his left hand flat on the floor, and the floor comes up about a second and a half before it is under his hand.** She had four words available and **did not say them**, because a word in that room is a thing four people can agree about later and she cannot read the hand. He looks at the blistering, says *that ground is hot*, looks at her arm, and asks **one question — did you do that** — **and she says the word.** *Yes.* He says he has a bundle two miles up the road and has not got a hand that closes. **She carries it, because it cannot be carried in one hand. Fifty minutes, two miles, and it did not make the hand better, and he tells her he is not going to think about it again.** And that night the ring stands at a finger's width and stops, six times in an hour and a half. **The plain part: every true thing she had said for six months had gone into a book, and the first true thing she has said since a strip went east went into nobody's book at all.** |
| 379 | **915** | the **2nd** | The Gate On The Made Road | **MOUTH TWO OF THE STRUCTURAL ANSWER, in a gate man sixty with forty acres on one side of a gate a hundred yards short of the post.** She walks a mile and a half past the counting house on the mill road for the first time in seven months. **Four or five sentences, short: it is kept so that this ground stays worth farming; forty acres here are worth what forty acres here are worth because of a thing he has never seen and could not get to; he has watched them go past twice a day for forty years and has never stopped one; nobody has come down that road in sixty years asking him what it is for; he is not going to ask her why she is walking, and there is a bench under a sycamore that is not his.** **She does not sit on the bench.** **She does not touch the book on the stone under his ladder, and she sees that somebody has taken it up and put it down again and put a rag over the corner of it.** Two nights of not sleeping, and thirty-one days on the ring. Yuan, one sentence. |
| 380 | 916–918 | the 3rd–5th | The Floor Above The Counter | **The copying-floor card, and the man with the brush appears once.** She **spends her one question a month** — is there anybody on the floor above yours — and is told there is a stair, that there is no man at the top of it with a list, and that she has been standing at the bottom of it for six months looking at the top of it. **He tells her what is up there in one breath: more open book in one day than has come into that building in the four years he has been on it.** She works the depth problem at the gate, finds the bar will not hold at three and a half on the low side, and hides it from Pei because she has already spent his one sentence. **She does not go up the second flight that day, because a woman who walks up a stair with nothing in her hands in a building with nine men in it gets read — and she will go up it with something in her hands.** A ditch line arrives on the works board. Yuan, one sentence. |
| 381 | 919–921 | the 6th–8th | Forty Thousand Columns | **THE THIRD FLOOR, THE LARGEST SET OF OPEN BOOKS SHE WILL STAND IN FRONT OF, AND RAO.** She goes up nine steps with her strip in her left hand. Forty books open at once on a table the length of nine men. **Rao: about forty-five, four feet ten, a thumb gone off at the first joint, sixteen years, forty thousand columns read.** Three ordinary questions about columns, answered correctly, and then eleven words in the ordinary voice: **there is no line on this floor.** **Her hands go warm in that room and have been cold on every road since the second of a month, and she works out in ten seconds that it is not the place — it is whether she is the only living thing in the room.** Rao gives her her own name, asks for hers, and does not ask twice. **She says twenty-nine dates out loud on the eighth and is quicker than a boy of twenty-two, and is made to do one date twice to find out whether she is reading it or getting it out of the shape.** The plain part: she is not angry about the no, and that is new. |
| 382 | 922–923 | the 9th–10th | The Smallest Person On The Floor | **Rao in depth, and the first time anybody has asked her for anything in seven months.** She is made to read a yard column out loud, thirty days, and told she is not reading it — she is getting it out of the shape. **The last line is in a hand neither she nor Rao can read, and Rao has asked twice in sixteen years for somebody who can and been told there was nobody to send.** At the fifth hour a boy of sixteen puts a bundle of strips on the table and goes down. **Rao cannot carry it down nine steps and up nine steps, and there is a rail on the landing with a turn in it, and she says outright that she is not going to be thanked for it.** Ning Yan says she cannot carry it, then says she can, and then says the true thing: **she can do it once and cannot do it nine times running, and she is not going to promise the second.** She does it in four minutes. **She will carry that bundle on the first of every month and there is still no line on that floor with her on it.** Pei is right about her being lighter and says a man at a gate is allowed one thing. |
| 383 | **924** | the **11th** | **NASCENT SOUL 3** — A Line In Her Own Hand | [Ning Yan, past the line, servant-born, under no ceiling any county holds. Nascent Soul, stage three, is held.] **Taken at the seventh hour on the top floor, standing at the edge of a table with a pencil in her hand, in a room with the door shut and nothing else alive in it** — thirty-eight days after NS 2, and she chose the room. **Rao comes in ten minutes after with an empty cup, asks once, and is told once.** She says four things: she is the only person on that ground whose trade is paper and pencil; **she is not going to write it down**; she is not going to pretend she does not know what it is; and a woman who has been at a table for sixteen years can look at a room and not write down what she saw in it. **Then the cost, found out on the bottom margin of a leaf in a quarter of an hour: she sets up to write a seven where the column says a four, and her hand writes a four. Five times, fast and slow and upside down.** **A line she puts in ink in her own hand is a thing she means; she can still say a false thing out loud; she can no longer enter a false line in any book on that ground, and there is no hand she can pay to enter one for her.** She carries the leaf home folded in four and does not burn it. Yuan, one sentence. |
| 384 | 925–928 | the 12th–15th | What Her Hand Would Not Do | **The drain is solved and it is the first thing she has fixed in six weeks: four feet for the first six feet and three and a half for the last fourteen, so the bar goes in where the ground is hard and the low side stays where Pei said twice.** She goes back up and tells Rao she cannot put a wrong date in a margin, and Rao says the floor does not check hands and asks her to work that out for herself rather than take it from a woman of forty-five with a thumb gone. **A margin correction on that floor is four coppers, it is four for everybody, and she has eight — and she works out in a minute that the reason is not money: a line she did not write is a line she cannot check, and two lines came off a column nine days east five weeks ago.** She could have paid it and does not take it. **Thirty-one days on the ring, and one fire in fifty-two.** |
| 385 | 929–932 | the 16th–19th | Ten Coppers For A Day | **THE FIRST OFFER OF A LINE ENTERED FOR HER BY SOMEBODY ELSE, IN THE ORDINARY HAND, FOR A FEE — and it is not a villain's offer.** The man behind the counter points at the shut seasonal book and says that book is what people said in this room, and if she wants a line of her own in it then it has to be said at the counter and written by him. **Ten coppers, which is a day at that counter and ten days at Pei's gate.** She has eight. **She cannot pay it and she cannot take it, and she says the two numbers and does not say no to anything.** Then the escalation, on the page, known to nobody: **before the eleventh of a month there were three places inside nine days of road where four coppers would buy a false line in a book in the ordinary hand. From the eleventh of a month there are none** — not hers, and not anybody's, because the four coppers and the wanting do not live in the same place in a person. **And the plain part: the boards at the second hour are not a book and nothing reads them, and that is the whole of what is left to her of writing.** |
| 386 | 933–936 | the 20th–23rd | The Ditch Line Is Listed | **THE ACTION CARD AND THE DEADLINE. A ditch line at the north end of the yard at the turn, by the length, four feet, four hundred yards, on the works board since the thirteenth with the twenty-third in the margin, because the low side of that ground goes at the end of a month.** **The man with the rule in his hand does not put her on it and says so twice, then asks her for the first time — on the twentieth, a day early, and says he is telling her now so it is not a thing that happened to her tomorrow.** She is in the cut on the twenty-first and the twenty-second with her right arm open the whole way. **At the fifth hour on the twenty-first the north face comes in on her from above and left, about a yard and a half of wet clay in a second and a half, with her right arm between it and the wall.** She gets out on her knees with her left hand in four seconds and the arm goes into the other thing for two seconds and comes back. She sits on the bank for ten minutes and then gets back down and finishes the last hundred and sixty yards with one hand. **Off on the twenty-second, a day early, checked, and rubbed off his own board with his own thumb. She is not on the board and there is no name of hers in that yard.** |
| 387 | 937–940 | the 24th–27th | Spent On Nothing | **The arm does not come back: she can get four fingers and a thumb halfway to the palm on the twenty-third, shut them nine times on the twenty-fourth, and cannot shut them at all on the twenty-fifth.** **MERIT SETTLES ONCE, on the listed ditch clearing, not on a death. The share is three days and six hours, told out loud twice before it is spent, in the present tense, with his reason for saying it twice** — and spent out of her foundation on nothing. **The price is a day and a half of cold hands on the top floor with a woman in the room, and on the twenty-sixth they are warm again: it was one afternoon and not the beginning of anything.** Pei looks at the new shape of the drain, says he has had his one sentence, and goes for his cup. **And at the table on the twenty-seventh Rao asks her what she comes up there for, and she says it, and Rao says no, and then says: you have got until the fifth hour.** She goes down with a day left and two words in her head. |
| 388 | **941–942** | the **28th–29th** | **The Column With Her In It** | **THE BATCH'S IRREVERSIBLE STEP. SHE SAYS A PERSON'S NAME OUT LOUD IN A ROOM AND ASKS FOR SOMETHING, AND IT IS A YES.** She is sick about it twice on the road and goes anyway. **“Rao.”** — the first name she has said out loud on that ground in seven months. She asks for four hours a month in that room on a day she names, gives the reason first (her hands, which are cold on every road and not cold in that room when Rao is in it) and then, when told that is not the whole of it, **the whole of it: something comes up out of her when there is another living thing in the room, and does not when there is not.** **Rao says yes in eleven words and rules out being paid, thanked or honoured before it happens, and sets the terms: the fourth hour on the sixth, eighth, eleventh and fourteenth, and the bundle down nine steps on the first.** Then she puts the stair book on the table — who went up the nine steps, on what day, at what hour, sixteen years of it — and says she is eight lines down it and has written *a woman* eight times because she was not given a name. **“Ning Yan. Of Lowwater.”** **Rao writes it in the ordinary hand and says it goes up the mill road on the first with a cord round the tray and is copied out on the ninth by a man who cannot read her hand and cannot read the fine hand either, and that nobody inside nine days of road will be able to tell her what is in it.** *It was a yes before the name, and I would have taken the yes without it.* On the twenty-ninth the man behind the counter says it out loud to her and there is no hand in that building that can take it out. |
| 389 | **950** | the **7th** | Fifteen Coppers And No Bread | **THE LAST PAGE.** The money, said out loud and done twice on the boards: **ten at the end of the thirtieth, eleven on the first, thirty-six days at the gate at a copper a day, four trips west at eight, fifteen at the last page** — the first month since she came past this mill she has finished with more than she started with. **She walks five miles west on the sixth with fifteen coppers, looks at four loaves at two coppers, and walks out**, and is going back in the morning because there is a bundle on the eighth. On the sixth, at the fourth hour, the boy of sixteen says good morning to her on his way down nine steps. **And the wood of the table is warm under her hand and Rao puts her pencil down flat and says *the wood is warm*, and then does not talk about it, and lights the stove.** The ring stands at a finger's width for the fortieth day. **The plain part: her hands go cold again about a hundred yards past the counting house, at a gate on the mill road she walks past four times a month and has never spoken to.** Then the hook: **on the first of a month that stair book goes up the mill road with a cord round the tray, and on the ninth a man with a brush — who has never been able to read the fine hand — is going to copy a name into another book in a hand he can read, and she cannot pay anybody to read it back to her.** *You put it there yourself.* |
