| 390 | **951** | the **8th** | Forty Thousand And One | **THE VOLUME'S MIDPOINT, AND IT IS A REVERSAL THAT COSTS HER NOTHING.** Fifteen coppers and no bread and she goes up the mill road instead of walking west. **Rao reads her column back to her, all of it, in the ordinary voice, in a room** — her name in a hand Rao cannot read, five months of days and hours, a season marked at the first end and **not marked at the second end**, a place four miles east in the ordinary hand, a rule underneath with nothing at the end of it, and at the foot of the page dated the twenty-sixth, in the ordinary hand of that building: *Ning Yan, of Lowwater. The holder is not presented. Refused at the counter. The twenty-sixth.* **It says no. It is the only refusal in that set and nobody on that floor has ever had one to read, and Rao has read forty thousand columns and has never read one back to the person it belongs to.** **The strongest thing in this building is a column.** Said once, followed by *I am not going to say it a second time.* **Then the turn: every room on nine days of road has priced her right and every price has been for the hands and the arm and four feet of a bar.** **Her own sentence about a room: everything that has ever been done to her came out of a book in a room and she has never once been in the room while it was happening.** The second line at the head of the column is a name and is not read. |
| 391 | 952–954 | the 9th–11th | The Rule With Nothing At The End | **MOUTH THREE OF THE STRUCTURAL ANSWER, in Rao, four sentences and no more:** what is on the line is paid for out of a place she has never seen and has never had a road to; a column came up off that line once in eleven years with nothing at the end of the rule and she has never found out what that meant; the room it is paid out of is not on any strip she has ever been sent; and whoever sends it has never once sent anything to ask. **The tenth part is not given.** And the answer to it: an office has been sitting on an unanswered column for eleven years and has not given up on it. **At the yard on the tenth she watches a man rub eight lines off a board and one of them is a sill line with no name on it.** And **the fire comes up in her own shed on the eleventh, where it has stood at a finger's width for forty days, and stands higher than it has stood since before she came** — and she stands in a doorway and understands something came up under the brick while nothing else in nine days of road was near the floor. She cannot work it out that night. She works it out on the eighteenth, in a yard, and it costs a stone that cannot go back. |
| 392 | 955–957 | the 12th–14th | Thirty-One Dressed Stones | **A NEW LINE ON THE WORKS BOARD: a sill course at the south end of the yard at the turn, by the weight, thirty-one dressed stones, four feet of bed, off the twenty-eighth**, because the low side of that ground goes at the end of a month. She reads it eleven times and works out what a sill is by walking east and looking at every building between the shed and the turn. **The man with the rule in his hand says the date out loud and says he is not putting her on it and is not going to, and says she will not be paid anything for any part of it** — and *you have not asked me for anything, and that is why I am saying the date.* **At the south end, two men and a bar and a chain and a boot get a dressed stone up half an inch four times.** **On the fourteenth she reads a column out loud with a yard at the turn in it and it is not hers.** The plain part: she wanted her hours on that page, was ashamed of it inside a minute, and did it anyway. And she works it out at a field gate that both of her two things are true and there is no third thing to put between them. |
| 393 | 958–959 | the 15th–16th | Two Men Short | **She watches the stone fail five times and works out that she cannot do any part of that line.** The man with the rule in his hand says to the board that he has nine men and the sill course wants four and he has four. **On the sixteenth, at the tenth hour, a bar goes, and a man's leg is under a dressed stone for about nine seconds and comes out in four.** And she stands nine feet off and works out two things in two seconds: that the bar will go again before the twenty-eighth, and **that the stone will not lie square afterwards, because the near end of the bed will have a lip on it and the far end is riding on a plank.** The man says three things out loud so that nine men hear them, including *you were standing nine feet off and you did not come in, and that was correct, and I would say it to anybody.* **The plain part: she wanted to say one ordinary sentence to a man about a stone, and did not, because there is no version of it that would not have gone into a yard with nine men on it.** Yuan, three sentences at the second hour on the boards: *you are going to put it in a room.* |
| 394 | **960** | the **17th** | **A Bed With A Lip On It** | **NASCENT SOUL 4, bracket 3 of six, at the seventh hour in the open at the south end of that yard with nine men on it and two of them nine feet off her.** Thirty-six days after NS 3. [Ning Yan, past the line, servant-born, under no ceiling any county holds. Nascent Soul, stage four, is held.] **This is the one chapter that is not a bracket chapter and is where she hears the last four words of it and finds they have stopped being true**, because there are men inside nine days of road who are three and four stages above her. **At the eighth hour a bar goes and a man's leg is under the stone and she has two seconds, and she PUTS IT THERE ON PURPOSE.** It goes the length of her own arm and stops. **It goes sideways and nine inches down into the open ground on the low side.** Nine men see it. Short breath five counts and back to four in a minute. **The man with the rule in his hand cuts ninety feet off his own line with his own chalk and says: that is going in the yard book, and it goes up the mill road on the first and comes down on the ninth, and it is not my book and it does not stay in that yard, and there is no column on this ground for what is written at the foot of a page.** **A thing she reaches cannot be put back where it was.** She wanted it to go up. She was sick at the office end at two hundred yards. |
| 395 | 961–968 | the 18th–25th | Nine Men Going Round A Hole | **The line restarts at the north corner and there are four dressed stones in the low side and about two hundred yards of work that four men have walked round.** She uses it four more times in eight days on purpose. **The fire comes up under a man's boot at the sixth hour on the twenty-second and she cannot turn it off**, and the man puts a square of plank down where he was standing and says nothing. **The man with the rule in his hand, twenty-two years keeping that yard: I am going to write it on Tuesday, that is the day the book goes up the mill road, and it comes back down on the ninth. And there is nothing on this ground I can put in the margin of that page which will explain it, and I am not going to invent a term for it.** He is right about all three. **She wanted to say *write my name on it*, and worked out that a gift at that yard goes into a page, and a page goes up the mill road on the first, and she has spent six weeks learning what that does to a person.** The plain part: proud for two hundred yards and then sick for four miles. |
| 396 | 969–974 | the 26th–30th, then the 1st | **The Cord Round The Tray** | **MERIT SETTLES ONCE, on the listed sill course, off the board at the fourth hour of the twenty-eighth, checked by the man with the rule and two of his men and rubbed off with his own thumb.** Twenty-nine stones instead of thirty-one. **THE SHARE IS FOUR DAYS AND TWO HOURS, said out loud twice at the second hour on the boards before it is spent**, not off a death, and the yard will not pay anybody for it. **She spends it on nothing and the fire goes off and does not come back for four days** — the twenty-ninth, the first, the second, the third — and on the sixth, at the fourth hour, on the top floor of the only building inside nine days of road with open books in it, **the back of her left hand is cold against cold wood for the first time in nineteen sittings, and a woman of forty-five says *Wood's cold*, and turns the page.** **ON THE FIRST, AT THE SIXTH HOUR, THE TRAY CORDS UP AND GOES UP THE MILL ROAD** with her name in it and a yard-book page with a hole in it and nothing in the margin. She stood in a gateway a hundred yards short of the counting house and wanted to run four miles after it and did not. Yuan, three sentences: *you have spent it.* |
| 397 | 975–980 | the 2nd–7th | A Foot And A Half Down | **SHE TRIES TO PUT IT BACK AND IT OPENS A SECOND HOLE.** Four miles for nothing, four minutes over a stone, her hand four feet above it, and it goes sideways and a foot and a half down into the loose three feet from the bed. **Four men on the bed say *they've put another one in there* and *aye, that's the third*, without turning round.** On the third, the man with the rule in his hand counts the four stones twice with his mouth and fills the second hole with two barrow-loads of clay and stamps it down with his heel and does not look at her. **On the sixth, at the third hour, the fire comes back under the edge of a plank and she puts her foot on it to stop it and it comes up through the plank four inches instead.** **At the second hour on the seventh she works out the difference: the fire moves when she asks.** The five months of merit came back on their own. A thing her hand reaches does not. And she wants to know what is in the stair book, and works out that on the ninth she will be at a gate, because the gate pays a copper and there is nothing else that pays. |
| 398 | 981–985 | the 8th–12th | Eleven Years Of Clay | **THE NINTH: THE TRAY COMES DOWN, AND SHE MISSES IT BY A QUARTER OF AN HOUR.** A man with a brush comes down three steps with the book open and the door shut behind him: there is a woman's name in it with a day and an hour and **not one word about work**, and it is the only line in sixteen years with more than one word at the left of it, **and it is now in two books on nine days of road instead of one, and both of them shut, and there is no hand in that building that can take a line out of either.** *You gave her the name yourself.* **MOUTH FOUR OF THE STRUCTURAL ANSWER, in a woman of fifty who has driven clay up a track for eleven years, between one barrow-load and the next:** there is a household nine miles west of me that has paid me in clay every month for eleven years; it is not in this county and I do not know anybody in it; I have never seen the house; I go where the clay goes and I come back; and it has never once been late. **On the eleventh, at the fourth hour, Rao says *it came back on the ninth* and *it is the emptiest line I have ever written in*, and says she is not going to take it out and is not going to be thanked or honoured by it.** |
| 399 | 986–989 | the 13th–16th | The Book Of What Happened | **THE YARD BOOK CAME BACK DOWN THE MILL ROAD WITH TWO LINES AT THE FOOT OF IT.** *Nothing on this page can be entered as it stands. A holder is required against the seventeenth of a month and the holder is not at this end of it.* He cannot do one thing with them and says he is sending the page up again on the first with the two lines still on it, **and is going to write in the margin of his own page, in his own hand, that the two lines came down off the ninth and were not in the page when it went up.** Then the worst thing: one of his nine asked him whether the woman from the gate was the one who did it, and he said the same three words he says about four hundred things, and *that is also the answer that puts you in it.* **She wanted to say *tell him* and did not, and walked four miles without a word.** On the fifteenth, the man who asked gives her a bucket of water at seven feet off and says four words and asks her nothing at all. |
| 400 | 990–992 | the 17th–19th | The Line With One Name On It | **A STRIP COMES DOWN THE MILL ROAD AT THE EIGHTH HOUR OF THE EIGHTEENTH AND IT IS NOT THE FIRST AND IT IS NOT THE NINTH.** The man behind the counter says it is about the line he has had in that book for five months and has never been able to do one thing with, and that **it has now been sent for.** Two lines: the room was empty on the twenty-eighth five months running, and the line is to be carried or closed, and a holder is required against it. **He cords the tray and does not ask, and he takes it up nine steps because that is where a holding is kept.** Rao reads it standing up in eleven seconds and twice, and says she has kept a holding twice in sixteen years and both were a place and not a person, and **there has never been a page come down off the road with a word at the bottom of it from a floor above this one.** She asks, at four feet, in the ordinary voice: **is there a person on this ground who is that holder.** And Ning Yan says no, there is one name on it and it is not hers and he is not on this ground. **Rao writes nine words at the top of it in her own hand and says she is not going to be thanked.** **And at the second hour on the nineteenth, on the boards, out loud to an empty room, she names the price before it is spent: a holder's answer is paid out of the foundation of the person it is about.** She says it twice, because it is a thing she wants to hear twice. And then she says he will not do it, and that she knows that the way she knows her own hands. |
| 401 | 993–994 | the 20th–21st | **The Place An Answer Goes** | **THE THIRD FRAME SCENE, away from her hearing, in the layer's own reading-space, and it is the book's protagonist's act.** Not the field of ch 272, not the room with two shut doors of ch 338 — and the chapter says so in its first two lines. **A narrow room with a low ceiling and no furniture, and one strip of paper on the floor nine feet off.** Two lines on it in a hand he has never seen. **Being held is a thing that can be answered now and he has known that for four months and has not answered anything, because there was nothing to answer.** He stands with it for nine seconds and then turns toward it. **He looks for the morning. It is not there. He looks for the unnamed state. It is not there. All three of his prices stay gone and none of them is given back.** **THE THIRD THING, AND IT IS WORSE THAN THE SECOND:** being answerable is not being findable and it is not being sendable — a finding can be denied, a sending can be refused once, and an answer cannot be refused at all, **because an answer is the only one of the three that is a thing anybody can quote.** **It goes out of the ground she is standing on, under her feet, at the second hour on the nineteenth, and not out of her hands.** He has felt the one before, at a counter, and did not know whose it was, and did not ask. **He does not tell her, and he does not do it twice, because one is what he has and two would be a habit, and a habit is a thing that gets found.** |
| 402 | 995–996 | the 22nd–23rd | **A Question About Her** | **THE BREAK, AND THE BILL.** On the twenty-second the man behind a counter comes out of the door in his coat and reads it to her **on the step, in daylight, because he will not read it over a counter with the book open.** The same strip: *The holder has answered. The holder has been read to and the reading has been carried up and this ground has not had that happen before. The answer is given.* And under it: *The answer names a woman refused at this counter on the twenty-sixth of a month six months back. Is that woman at the gate three miles east of the mill.* **She had nine days to think about this and used eight on other things, and she was not surprised, because she named the price out loud on the boards on the nineteenth and got it right.** She says **yes**, in the ordinary voice, in daylight, at four feet, and he writes eleven words in the seasonal book, which shuts: *Ning Yan, of Lowwater. At the gate three miles east of the mill. The twenty-second.* **He tells her on the step that the refusal of the twenty-sixth is still in that book and nothing has come off it and he is not touching it, and that she has now given that counter a second thing of her own, and that it shuts.** **THE PLAIN PART: she can never again refuse a reading. Not because there is a rule and not because anybody has decided it — because an office on this ground now holds an answer that has already been given, and an office that holds an answer already given does not accept a second one. There was one option in that book and she spent it six months back and had it still until the eighth hour that morning.** She was not got: she stood four hundred yards down the made road and made it nine times. And the yard page goes up the same road with **two holders required against the seventeenth of a month and there is one holder on this ground.** |
| 403 | 997–998 | the 24th–25th | Asking To Take A Name Out | **SHE ASKS, OUT LOUD, IN A ROOM, FOR HER NAME TO BE TAKEN OUT — AND IT IS NOT GRANTED AND IT IS NOT REFUSED.** On the twenty-fourth, which is not a day of the four. *Rao. Take my name out of the stair book.* **Both**, she says, and cannot pay for it. Rao tells her the shape of it in four sentences: those books went off this floor for eight days and she does not know what was done to them, and the only person who has ever had a page of hers in his hands without her is the man who cords the tray, and he does not read what is on it. **I told you on the twenty-eighth of a month that there was no hand in this building that could take it out. I was not being kind to you and I am not being kind to you now.** **The plain part: the first thing she ever asked anybody for was granted in eleven words, and the second was not, and there is a difference and she cannot tell which one she is holding.** Three men at the yard have read the margin note. **At the eighth hour on the twenty-fifth the woman in the office end puts a cup of something hot into her hands for the twelfth time and she did not want it, and she has not worked out what that is.** |
| 404 | **999** | the **26th** | **A Cup Across A Bench** | **NASCENT SOUL 5, bracket 4 of six, at the seventh hour on the north end of the yard at the turn, in the open, in front of nine men**, thirty-nine days after NS 4. It came up under a forty-foot clay stack and through the strip of plank a man had laid down over the place where his boot had been. **COST, NAMED ONCE: she cannot be given anything. A gift enters the column and the column can be read. She can still be owed. She can still refuse to pay.** **THE THIRTEENTH CUP. The woman in the office end comes out and puts the hand round it and holds it out and her hand goes toward it and stops four inches short.** *Take it.* She does not take it. *All right.* The woman goes back in and the cup stands four inches from her right hand in the open air with nine men on the bed and a second course going on over a sill that is holding. She stands a minute and a quarter and puts it on the edge of the bench, which is where things go at that yard, **and watches it stop being hot.** **She had wanted it every one of the thirteen times and had not once thought about where it went.** Then the twenty-seventh to the thirtieth, the tray going up on the first with a cord round it at the sixth hour, and the threads that are still open. **THE LAST OF IT: she cannot be given anything, so there is nothing left that somebody can hand her that does not end up in a column — and that is the second time in about a month that she has worked out what she is for and found out it is a page.** |

## Volume 7 Batch 0004 — *Stronger Things*, ch 405–420, day 1,021–1,090 — WRITTEN

**The last batch of Volume 7. Thirty thousand words of body prose over sixteen chapters, average ~1,899, twelve of the
sixteen inside the 1,800–2,700 guide and four under it — ch 410 at 1,759, ch 413 at 1,770, ch 419 at 1,777, ch 418 at
1,783 — and none under the 1,300 floor. Two bracketed layer lines, at ch 412 and ch 420, which are brackets 5 and 6 of
the volume's six in sixty. The volume's own refusal is at ch 417 and it is the second irreversible decision in the
volume. The action card is ch 419–420: a bar, a chain, a man under a dressed stone and a bell that has already
gone.** The chapters were drafted by an earlier run, deferred, and repaired; the repair list is at the foot of the
`Volume 7 Batch 0004` block in `state/continuity.md`.

| Ch | Days | Month-day | Title | What happens |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 405 | **1,021** | the **18th** of the month beginning 1,004 | **The Weight Or The Length** | **THE BOARD FILLS.** Twenty-nine lines on a rail that stood at eight for a year and a bit, and one rule at the head of it eleven words long: *everything on this board is measured by the weight or measured by the length. Nothing on it is measured by the voice.* Two hundred and six yards of dressed stone, forty feet of bed, nine hundred feet of drain, two hundred yards of hedge, and a cart track in a different ink at the bottom. **She could take none of them.** The listing man tells her what the board is and refuses to say the thing she wants him to say. Five coppers, bread for three days, the store five miles west, and the gate on the seventh to the ninth hour. **She has been wrong about which one she wanted, and she worked it out standing at the end of a drain run in nine seconds.** |
| 406 | 1,022–1,025 | the 19th–22nd | **Two Prices For A Line** | The man with a rule in his hand walks four miles east and reads his own line. **Two prices on this ground and people mix them up the way they mix up a load and a column:** the rail is priced off the ground, and a line of her own in a book is four coppers on the third floor and ten at the counter, and it has never bought a person a place on that rail. She gives him the reason she has not paid the four, and it is the only reason she has: **there is no hand inside nine days of road that can write a line of hers and read it back to her.** He tells her, with his hand flat on the rail, where the one man who reads the fine hand stands among the people on that ground, and never asks who. The fourteenth cup at the yard goes on the bench and her hand does not do it at all. **A rule is not worked out twice, and she says it about a thing she wanted for the first time.** |
| 407 | 1,025–1,031 | the 22nd–28th | **Three Carts Going East** | **WEI RAN, twenty, three carts, four men, a whinny whip folded twice**, and a bank nine hundred feet east of the turn that goes in to the axle in a wet month. **Four coppers a day, said before she was asked, which is what a trade does.** She cuts it in two days and gives up the gate on both and works out twice that two coppers is what the choice cost. On the first evening four coppers go on a plank bench and **her hand goes out about the length of her own arm and stops a foot short and comes back down.** The second try goes eleven inches and stops. **Wei Ran picks them up, writes nothing, and says the rate does not move and that if she can work her for nothing she will use her for nothing, which is a worse rate than the four.** Eight coppers she has not got, and she is not hungry and has not been since before she came past the last working point. |
| 408 | 1,030–1,033 | the 27th–30th | **A Margin In His Own Hand** | Nine men on the north end, a second course going on over a sill that is holding, and a page off the mill road and back with **two lines under his margin note in a hand none of them can read.** She reads twenty-nine lines out loud and four men turn round and listen. He will not send the two lines anywhere; it goes up to a man and comes back readable, and it has been that way since before the wheel stopped. What he wants instead is the twenty-nine lines read out loud twice a month at the sixth hour, for nothing, off the board. **And then he writes four words on the margin in chalk, in his own hand, under his own hand, and turns the page round: *read by a person at the rail.*** That is not a line and not a column and not four coppers. **It goes up the mill road on the first at the sixth hour with a cord round the tray and comes back down on the ninth and a man with a brush will copy it into a book that shuts.** It is the first thing she has ever said in this world that is going into a book that shuts and she did not have to pay four coppers for it. **It closes forward, on the ninth of the next month: the page comes back down the mill road, four words in chalk go into a book on the floor below, and she is not going to be in the room when it happens** — which is where ch 414 opens and where ch 409 opens. *(Closed at review; the chapter had ended by looking back at five coppers it had already covered.)* |
| 409 | 1,034–1,035 | the **1st** and 2nd of the month beginning 1,034 | **Copied Upward** | The tray goes up at the sixth hour and comes down on the ninth and she has followed nothing up that road in seven months. On the copying floor, a man of forty-eight with eleven years on it. **MOUTH FIVE OF THE STRUCTURAL ANSWER.** Four sheets go up on the first of a month, have gone up since before he came, **and he does not make them** — they come off the cart on the second already written and he puts them in the bundle, and he has never seen the day before the second and does not know where on nine days of road they are written. It is a bundle of pages with a cord round it and it goes up in the open, and everything anybody on this ground has ever wanted to keep out of a book has got into it anyway. He has been asked twice in eleven years what the hand is and has said he does not know both times. **Four sheets of her on a table, going up, and she does not ask to read them.** Then a yard at a turn, four days east, where the fourth line is in chalk in his hand and there will be a thing in a book on this ground that a woman at a gate said out loud. |
| 410 | **1,036** | the **3rd** of the month beginning 1,034 | **Four Columns Off The Road** | Four columns off the road on the top floor for the first time since the wheel stopped. Rao has the same pencil and the same knife and forty books open. **A column is a day and an hour repeated for thirty days and then a season at the head of it, and it gets read out loud exactly twice in its whole life** — and one of the two is now her, on the fourth, the eleventh, the eighteenth and the twenty-fourth, at the fourth hour, for nothing. She finds a gap on the seventeenth with a man's mark and no hours under it, and gives Rao the correct reading of it three months late. **Then Rao finds the four words in chalk on the margin of a yard page off a cart, reads it without looking at the writing, and says out loud that she wants to stop her coming on the first of a month.** **She writes a slip herself, in her own hand, with a date and a place and what was said, puts it in beside a column, and charges her nothing and asks for nothing.** She has one piece now, she did not ask for it, she did not refuse it, and she cannot put it back. |
| 411 | 1,036–1,037 | the 3rd–4th | **What A Column Needs** | **She asks a man behind a counter for a line, out loud, in a room with eleven men in it, for the first time in seven months.** Ten coppers, and she has seven. **A line is three things: a name at the head of it, a first day, and a last day**, and the fourth is a place, and the place is the part that gets you. She has the name, said out loud on a step on the twenty-second. She has the first day, seven months of a drain at five coppers a reach. **She does not have the third one, and the third one is whether she will be at that gate in the spring, and inside nine days of road there is not a person she could ask.** He asks it anyway, and she cannot answer it with a sentence. **And the reason she did not get a line was never the ten, and she works that out at the third milestone in ninety seconds and then stands on a made road for eleven minutes working out how it had been the money.** |
| 412 | **1,037**–1,038 | the **4th** of the month beginning 1,034 | **No Last Day On It** | **[Ning Yan, past the line, servant-born, under no ceiling any county holds. Nascent Soul, stage six, is held.] — bracket 5 of six**, at the seventh hour, up through the floor of a drain run she cut four days ago, in front of eleven men on a bank, one of whom looked at the ring. **COST, NAMED ONCE: she cannot be paid.** At the eighth hour the man behind the counter shows her the page he opened on a day he does not usually open it, with a pen to it twice, and says the reason out loud: **a column carrying a person past nine has no last day on it and no figure against it, so above nine the hours go on as a running thing with nothing at the end of them to put a figure against.** Not laziness, not a missing name, not a missing first day. **He has been sitting there since the fourth hour working out whether he could do it and he cannot.** Four other counters within nine days of road and he will not tell her to try them, because she did not go to them for money. She can still have the four-copper line on the third floor and he is not going to pretend the two are the same. Then a board with thirty-four lines on it, none of which she can take, and a full rail she goes to every morning at the sixth hour because there is nowhere else to go at the sixth hour. **The bare board was a season. This is the rest of the run, and she shut the pay question herself and there is not a person in any of it to be angry at.** |
| 413 | 1,038–1,041 | the 5th–8th | **The Rest Of The Run** | A man of thirty at the north end asks, in the voice a man uses about somebody else, whether she gets paid for reading, and is answered in four words. She asks the man with the rule in his hand whether reading every month puts her in a book, and is told that a margin on a yard page is not a line and is not hers and is not a person. **He says three sentences and they are the volume's argument:** a person who works for nothing is not a line and not a column and not a day against anything, being able to read is not a thing this ground has ever paid anybody for, and the day she stops being surprised by that is the day she is standing in a room with nine men who already know she is not getting anything. Eleven men on a bank look at her for nine seconds in two hours. **And nine men on that ground have begun, with no arrangement, to rely on a thing she does that this ground has never paid anybody for, which is worse than being left out.** |
| 414 | 1,042–1,056 | the 9th–23rd | **Five Days And One Hour** | The tray comes down on the ninth and the four words go into a book that shuts and the man with the brush neither stops nor looks up. The hedge comes off the board and the man with a rule in his hand walks it and rubs it off with his own thumb. **Every line that comes off that rail pays a share to whoever was on the ground when it closed, and it is not money and it does not go into a purse. It is time. It is off the woman who was standing on the bed. This one is five days and one hour, and he says it twice, because the last woman who was told once argued it was four days and went into the next season a day short.** She does not know what it costs and says so out loud in an empty shed, which is the first sentence in seven months she has started and not finished. It comes out of her hands on the eleventh and does not come back for **twelve days** — nine tries, four objects, four men working within thirty feet — and on the twenty-third at the fourth hour, with her left hand flat on a table of forty open books, it goes four feet and stops, and a woman of forty-five says, without looking up, *Wood is cold.* **The chapter does not end on the turned page.** Five days and one hour was spent on nothing and it bought a barrow wheel, a stone, forty books and twelve days of her hand going out and nothing coming up — and it came back once, because a woman said a thing about wood. **She will be at that gate in the morning. She has no idea whether she will be at it in the spring**, and that is ch 411's unanswerable third thing restated as a cost. *(Closed at review; the ending was on a procedure.)* |
| 415 | 1,044–1,049 | the 11th–16th | **A Bench Long Enough For Nine** | Two miles up the made road, a hundred feet long, one room, and **the biggest room she has ever stood in.** She stands in the doorway and does not go in, because he said *look at a page* and nobody said *come in*. **Geng, fifty-one, nineteen years, runs it**, and looks at her arm for two seconds and does the arithmetic and says none of it. He has four men who can lift and not one who can read, and it has been the same in every room he has ever stood in, and he has never once been in a room where both were in the same person. **MOUTH SIX OF THE STRUCTURAL ANSWER:** he was put on a list in the second year he was in that building, on a piece of paper with a name on it, in a hand that was not his, in a room he has never been in, and **he has never been taken off it in nineteen years, and there is nothing in that building about it, and he went looking about four years in and decided he would not go looking again.** And he wants it written down that he stopped thinking about it, because the day he starts again he will get it wrong, because he will start again on the assumption that somebody is waiting. She carries one hundred and thirty-eight back on a page in her own hand and he finds that a number on his own sheet has been wrong since the second. |
| 416 | 1,048–1,051 | the 15th–18th | **The Man Whose Trade It Is** | A room at the end of the floor above the counter, one door, a lamp, a sloped table, and a man of forty-three who reads hands, and she has known about it since a strip came down the works' road and has never gone up. **That hand writes four times a year and writes fast, and it is the hand of a man who has been taught one thing and has never had to do a second one.** It comes down about nine times a year and eight of the nine are lists of places and dates. **THE PART HE WILL NOT DO, told before she asks:** there is one condition on everything he reads and nobody has ever broken it. *I read, and I say what it is, and the person it is about has to tell me it is right or tell me it is wrong.* Four people in this room in twenty-six years, and he tells her that. **Confirming a thing means entering it**, and she has known since the eighth of a month four months back that her hand cannot write a false line. She says thank you — the first thing she has said since the strip that was for another person. Then on the eighteenth at the yard at the turn **she gets two dates wrong in front of nine men and he does not correct her once, because a correction would have put her in a book**, and she tells the yard out loud where she got them right. |
| 417 | 1,051–1,053 | the 18th–20th of the month beginning 1,034 | **A Denial Is An Entry** | **THE VOLUME'S OWN REFUSAL, AND THE BATCH'S IRREVERSIBLE DECISION.** At the ninth hour of the night of the eighteenth a sheet comes down the mill road in the fine hand with a woman's name at the head of it, and it stops in a room with a lamp in it, and a man of forty-three reads it twice and does not sleep. She does not know. On the nineteenth at the seventh hour he says he is going to read it to her **once**, in that room, and then she has to tell him whether it is right or wrong, **and that is how a reading is finished.** She asks what happens if she says no, and is told the true thing: a reading does not sit in a room, it goes up, and **a person who will not hear a thing they have been offered in a room is the only person in eleven hundred years who has ever answered one without hearing it.** She asks where a confirmation goes, and where a denial goes, and what happens if she says no now, and is told that nothing is written, that no book in that building will not shut, that nothing fails. **She says six words. Nothing is written. A man of forty-three puts the sheet in the groove with the other two and goes back to the top of a sheet that was not that one.** She gave up a want she had carried since a strip came down a road, to avoid becoming a person who can be entered twice, and she did it in daylight with the door shut and no force anywhere in the world in it. |
| 418 | 1,053–1,066 | the 19th–25th, then the **1st–3rd** of the month beginning 1,064 | **What Six Months Costs** | **THE BILL.** A reading that has not been answered does not sit in a room. A boy of sixteen who has been on that floor two years and does not know what a column is stops in the middle of putting a roll down and asks her about carts on the mill road on the eighteenth. By the third of the next month four people on that floor ask her a question and write the answer down, and on the 29th the man behind the counter opens the book at the page with her name against the twenty-second and asks the one question she cannot answer: **is there anybody inside nine days of road who could read the hand it is in.** She does not answer and he shuts the book. **She would rather be entered.** One line in a book with a day against it is over and shut and she knows what it says. **This is a boy of sixteen asking her about carts on a stair, four hours a month, forever, with nothing written down anywhere at the end of that floor.** Then **THE HUNTER, once, nine feet off on a made road, notebook shut under his arm**: she asks whether an office can send for a person who is not standing on the ground, and he says yes, at no cost to either of them, in the ordinary voice. **It needs a line with your name at the head of it and a rule under it, and where you are standing is not a part of it. What it costs the person being sent for is not the road and not a room. It is the six months it will take you to hear about it.** She tells him there is one already, and he does not write it down, because he never writes anything she says down. |
| 419 | **1,075** | the **12th** of the month beginning 1,064 | **The First One Has Not Finished** | A question on that floor about a woman at a gate, the second in eleven hundred years to be asked twice, **and the way it goes away is that the holder answers it again.** She does not answer the counter man. At the second hour, on the boards, **THE HAND REFUSED ONCE, OUT LOUD.** *No.* She says they will send it again. **The reason he gives, in three sentences, in the present tense: a second use is a second time to be sent for, and the first one has not finished.** It finished on the twenty-second of a month two months back at the eighth hour on a step in daylight, and **what it has not finished is coming back down that road, and I am still in it.** He tells her the price before the answer, which is what every man on nine days of road has taught her to look for. Then **she stands on the lifting floor for the first time**, of herself, with no message behind it, and a man of fifty-one does not comment on it. She reads four hundred and six pounds of dressed stone out loud for two hours and gets two wrong. **She puts her hand on the oak four times and nothing happens once, and says so out loud in a room with nine men in it, and is not asked why.** A man at the far end says *Wood* and picks up the bar. |
| 420 | **1,076**–1,090 | the **13th**–27th of the month beginning 1,064 | **Two Columns On One Sheet** | **THE LIFT, NASCENT SOUL 7, AND THE COLUMN READ OUT LOUD — THE LAST CHAPTER OF VOLUME 7.** The same man, the same frame, the same bar, at the seventh hour, and the bar goes out of his hands sideways and the bell goes off on the floor and a dressed stone comes down on the leg of a man of thirty. **She has two seconds and she puts it there, and what made it go is that a man asked her.** It goes four feet and about nine inches down into the joint of the floor and stops. **[Ning Yan, past the line, servant-born, under no ceiling any county holds. Nascent Soul, stage seven, is held.] — bracket 6 of six.** Her short breath goes to five counts and stays there for a minute. **The man gets up and puts his hands in a water bucket and walks, and Geng says the floor is a block short now and that the stone is not going back into that frame and that this floor cannot put it back and does not want to, and tells the room to write it up.** Geng has no term for a stone that went four feet and stopped on its own. **He holds the sheet out at arm's length, across the whole width of the room, to the smallest person in it. She takes it in her left hand, because the right one has four fingers and a thumb on it and it is the wrong hand for a page, and nine men knew which hand she would use before she did it.** She reads it out loud in the ordinary voice, the way she said her name at a trestle and at a gate and at a counter. A third of the way down she stops him and puts her thumb on a line and makes him read it in both columns: **the day the ground changed and the day he heard about it, eleven days apart, both in his own hand.** **The other column has been empty for nineteen years.** She turns the sheet and puts her finger on the bell's own line — the second of the month on the left, today on the right, also eleven days — and says he has written the day he found out and never once the day it happened. **They write it down. She watches them write it down.** **THE COST, LAST, IN ONE PLAIN SENTENCE: the reach will not go out of a room she has not been asked into, and she has not been asked into this one, and she has been in it for two hours.** Then the fourteenth to the twentieth, what is still open, and the second hour of the twenty-seventh: **nine men watched it happen, a bell went off nine feet from where she was standing, a floor is a block short, and a date went into two columns because she read it out loud — and the only thing she can do about any of it is be asked.** |

**Volume 7 as a whole: ch 361–420, 115,342 words of body prose over sixty chapters, average ~1,922. The six brackets are
all spent, one at each stage, at ch 371, 383, 394, 404, 412 and 420. The six structural-answer mouths are all on the
page, at ch 365, 379, 391, 398, 409 and 415, and the tenth part is still not given and nothing proves the crews
wrong. The volume's three irreversible acts are a circuit broken at ch 344, an entry made at ch 358 and an answer
withheld at ch 417, and they are three different shapes.**

---

## Volume 7 closed — audited

**Volume 7, *Stronger Things*, ch 361–420, sixty chapters. Written in four batches, two of them reviewed and repaired
at chapter level and two not, and closed by an audit that wrote no prose.**
**`outline/volume-07.md` walked line by line against the page. `outline/series.md` and `outline/ending.md` were not
opened and `outline/volume-07.md` was not re-planned.**

**115,342 words of body prose over sixty chapters, average ~1,922** — the `#` title line and every `##` section
heading excluded. **By batch 27,114 · 28,296 · 29,552 · 30,380**, all four re-derived at this close and all four
reproducing exactly. Longest chapter ch 420 at 2,582; shortest ch 410 at 1,759. **Five chapters under the 1,800
guide — 385, 410, 413, 418, 419 — none under the 1,300 floor, none above the 2,700 guide.**
**420 `#` headings manuscript-wide, zero collisions, longest 32 characters.**

**Sixteen findings recorded and none repaired, because a close that repairs is a rewrite.** Four are canon breaches on
the page, three are internal contradictions, and seven are recorded figures that do not reproduce. Full table in
`state/current.md` under `## Volume 7 close audit — CLOSED`; the repaired-by-measurement corrections are in
`state/continuity.md` under `## Volume 7 close — repairs` and `## Volume 7 close — the volume as a whole`.

### The goal, and where it is answered

**Fixed by `outline/series.md` §9: *In this volume, Ning Yan must survive being the weakest person in every room she
walks into, or she learns that strength was never what she was taken for.***

**It is paid, and it is paid in a room and not in a paragraph.** At ch 390, the volume's midpoint, Rao — who has kept
the columns on the third floor for sixteen years, holds no furnace, cannot lift, has no stage anybody on that ground
would name, and has read forty thousand columns without once being asked what her own column says — reads Ning Yan's
column back to her, all of it, in the ordinary voice. **The column contains a refusal, and it is the only refusal in
the set, and nobody on that floor has ever had one to read.** What she has spent the volume trying to be paid for and
what the rooms have been pricing her at are two different things: she was taken for her column, the column's one
talent is that it can refuse, and **the strongest thing in the building is a column.** Said once, in Rao's mouth, on
the last page of ch 390, in six words: **“A yard is a room.”** Not repeated anywhere in the volume.

| **The three shapes in three consecutive volumes** | A circuit broken (ch 344) · an entry made (ch 358) · **an answer withheld (ch 417)**. Ch 417 says the difference on the page — *Nothing is written in this room today… No book in this building will not shut. Nothing fails* | PASS, and none is restated as another |
| **Wei Ran** | On the page once, at ch 407. Twenty, three carts, four men, a rate and an opinion. **The thing that happens to her is that she is owed eight coppers and cannot pay them, and her hand will not take them**, and she does it in a trader's mouth with Ning Yan doing the refusing | PASS |
| **The four pressures** | rooms bigger than she is · what she is worth in a room · the reach on purpose · **what it costs to be right**. No two consecutive, and none of the four is a tending, ration, hiring, crossing, audit, examination, hunt, signature, keeping, debt, family paper, ash or confinement | PASS |

### The volume's closing image

**The biggest room she has ever stood in, and she is the smallest thing in it, and she is reading.**

Nine men and a bench long enough for all of them, a chain, a bar, a dressed stone standing four feet from where it
stood and a bell that has already gone. A man who runs the floor has a sheet on the bench with nine days of his own
work on it in his own hand, and there is no term on that floor for what he has to write, so he holds it out at arm's
length to the smallest person in the room.

She takes it in her left hand, because the right one has four fingers on it and it is the wrong hand for a page. She
reads it out loud, in the ordinary voice, the way she said her name at a trestle and at a gate and at a counter, and
about a third of the way down it she stops a man who is going to be in a room in eleven days. She tells him the day
he has written there is the day the ground changed and not the day he heard about it, and that the day he heard about
it is eleven days later and it is already in the other column on the same sheet.

**They write it down. She watches them write it down.**

And the last line of the volume, in her own arithmetic, standing about nine feet off with an empty left hand:

> **And the only thing she could do about any of it was be asked.**

**Where the records are.** Audit table, sixteen findings and the protagonist answered in writing:
`state/current.md` under `## Volume 7 close audit — CLOSED`. Repairs (none) and every re-derived figure:
`state/continuity.md` under `## Volume 7 close — repairs` and `## Volume 7 close — the volume as a whole`. Threads
at ch 420 and what Volume 8 inherits live: `state/open-threads.md` under `## Volume 7 CLOSED at ch 420`.
**This close phase created no prompt and wrote no `.done` marker**, which `PHASE_SYSTEM.md` gives to the controller
alone.
