## VOLUME 12 BATCH 0003 — ch 691–705, *the firsts*, drafted **and repaired after review, then reviewed again and repaired again**. Day 2,141 to day 2,240. Fifteen chapters, **29,240** words of body prose on method B, average 1,949. **29,202 and 29,212 are both old-page figures and must not be quoted.**

**Four firsts of a month go past inside this band and not three: 2,144, 2,174, 2,204 and 2,234 — the third, the fourth, the fifth and the sixth. She is at the frame for one of them in nine years. The volume's payoff figure, ten days and twenty-one hours and thirty-six minutes, is ch 717 and is Batch 0004's; this batch derives the total she already has.**

- **ch 691, days 2,141–2,143, the twenty-eighth to the thirtieth, *What A Voice Cannot Be Checked Against*.** Three days inside a record that is being kept of her out loud, in which nothing goes wrong, **which is the difficulty.** She waits all morning on the twenty-ninth for him to put down one word that is not true and gives him three chances and takes none, and then goes looking for one in a place that has not been searched: **she tells him her arm is closed when it is open, on purpose, in daylight, in front of two hundred people, and he writes it down because it is what she said.** She says out loud at the ninth hour that the fifth term cannot be enforced by either of them, and that what she wanted was for him to be wrong about something, and that there is no instrument. **Sixty-four lines on the board and not one of the four men can read hers.** The first of the month beginning 2,144 is at the first hour tomorrow and she is two days of road east of the frame.
- **ch 692, day 2,144, the first, *The Slate Goes East*.** The third first comes and goes and she is at the north end of the rows, **standing, in daylight, with a bar on the ground at her feet**, and it is the second time in nine years she has missed one. She stands in a ditch on a made road for an hour and forty minutes and counts nine carts, all east, and works out in nine seconds that **the slate does not come this way**. At the second hour a cart stops at the north end, and a man of forty with a rope over his shoulder puts down a flat stone, and off the stone a **strip of pale paper nine inches long and two across**, and keeps his hand out flat until she has taken it, and does not get down, and does not ask her one question, and does not look at it again. **There is nothing written on it.** She decides out loud, standing, at the ninth hour: **at the first hour of the first of the month beginning 2,174 she will be at the frame, and she has known for twenty-four hours that she was going to say it, which is the first time in eleven years she has known a thing a day in advance.**
- **ch 693, days 2,145–2,147, the second to the fourth, *The Square In The Middle Of It*.** Three days taking a blank strip apart. **Not county paper: no chain lines, no laid lines, long rag fibre every way at once, two edges cut and one torn for half an inch and stopped.** A square the size of the flat of her hand pressed into the middle of it, darker and not wet and not ink and not grease. **A third heavier than county paper of its size, proved on the bar with her ring.** She gets the smell at the seventh hour — cold iron, old smoke, paper shut up in a room with no window open — **which she has smelled exactly twice in her life and both times were four flights above a copying floor.** A bundle is a square of oilcloth with a weight in it, and this is one, and it came off the top of that stair. **And then the arithmetic of it, in nine seconds: she cannot be given a shape, and a thing with nothing on it is not a shape, and there is no form in this county it can come on except the eight leaves against her ribs.** So somebody sent her a blank one and she is not entitled to ask anybody.
- **ch 694, day 2,148, *A Shape Is A Thing Kept*. THE EIGHTH FRAME SCENE, away from her hearing.** Fourteen days read from the outside and in order. **He is not asking whether a fragment can be known without being spent. He is asking what it costs to keep one.** The finding: **a shape is not held, a shape is kept** — and nobody is keeping hers, not the four men who cannot read it, not the county, not the building, **not her.** The only shape ever kept in eleven hundred years is one somebody wrote for themselves and then carried. **The price: he cannot hold it, because a thing he holds is findable and findable is what she agreed to, so the only way to keep it is to ask her to keep it** — and asking her is ch 609's rule turned round and pointed at him, **and there is no version where she can hear the asking and go back to before she heard it.** He has a first of a month in thirty days and cannot say which one and she does not tell a person things a day in advance. **He is going to ask her anyway.**
- **ch 695, days 2,149–2,151, the sixth to the eighth, *Three Days Of Not Saying It*.** A figure with a hole in it, and the hole is whether the day she went on a slate is counted as the first. **Two numbers about herself at one frame that differ by a convention and not one person in the county to settle the convention.** On the seventh a man of thirty in the fourth row comes and says *you have got a thing you are not saying*, and she gets four words out and then says *the light is going* at the second hour — **a false thing, in daylight, to get out of saying a true one, the first in nine years** — and she has to stand at the south end by herself and say so out loud to the frame. On the eighth he says the other half of it once: **a record of what you said has a hole in it the moment you stop saying things.** She works out that she has been using the ch 665 asking as a reason, **and there is no rule in this county that stops a woman from standing up in a field and saying a number about her own arm.** She decides not to say it until it is right, and goes and looks for paper.
- **ch 696, days 2,152–2,156, the ninth to the thirteenth, *Six Days Of One Sum*.** The instrument is the back of the strip, **with no name on it, on purpose: a thing with no name on it is not a thing anybody is on.** Sixteen lines a day on four days, eight on the fifth because her arm is open, one blot on the fifth line of the second day that she is not going to take out. **Eighty days counted with the first day as the first, one in twenty, checked three ways — up from the bottom, down from the top, and apart into months.** 80 ÷ 20 = four. And one more line, and a day out of twenty is an hour and twelve minutes. **Then the thing that costs her the rest of the day, in nine seconds: it is not a secret. There is no craft in it. Any person in that ground who can count to eighty can have it this morning, and there are forty, and she has just told them there is one.**
- **ch 697, day 2,157, the fourteenth, *The Only Person Who Can Say It*. MIDPOINT, CLIMAX AND THE BATCH'S IRREVERSIBLE STEP.** She writes the eighty-first line in the dark in a count of forty and says it out loud once to herself, and then waits for the light, because she does not do this in the dark. She writes the sum in figures **and in words**, on purpose, so a person who cannot do the arithmetic can check it. At the ninth hour, standing, in daylight, at the north end of two hundred rows of stones: **my name is Ning Yan, I am nineteen, I have been on a slate at a turn two days of road west of this ground since the ninth hour of the twenty-fourth of the month beginning 2,054, and that is eighty-one days, and this county takes one in twenty, and it has taken four days and one hour and twelve minutes off me, and that is my own total, and it is the first one that has ever been said out loud in front of anybody.** **Nothing happened.** A man of forty in the ninth row asks how she knows; she says because if she tells him how there will be two hundred people who can do it; **he says *that is a very fine way of saying you found it first*, and she says yes.** Then: *there are four hundred and ninety people in this county who can count to eighty and I have never once counted anything about myself, because there was nothing to count. You have just told me there is.* **And it is out and it is true and there is not one instrument that can take it back.**
- **ch 698, days 2,158–2,164, the fifteenth to the twenty-first, *The Question She Asked Was Wrong*.** Nothing was taken off her; a first is for that. **And the figure moves.** A man of fifty asks whether his two rows are counted right; a woman of thirty-four asks about a store; a boy of seventeen asks whether four thousand and ninety stones at two to a cart is a whole number of carts; **by the twenty-first there is a queue of small sums forty feet long at the frame and three of them are not waiting for her.** On the seventeenth a man of thirty-three asks *is it the same for everybody* and works out in nine seconds that **a figure is the day your name goes on a board and one in twenty of every day since, and it does not stop** — and then asks what he has got to do about it, **and she has no answer and has had seven days since.** So she gives the answer to her own ch 665 question out loud in front of nine people: **no, you cannot, and the question was wrong — I wanted a figure without a name, and that is exactly what happened on the fourteenth.** And she refuses four times to tell any of them their own. **A hundred and forty-four lines on the board.**
- **ch 699, days 2,165–2,170, the twenty-second to the twenty-seventh, *What Thirty Days Costs*.** **Planning is not counting, and she has never done one before.** Six days measuring a bar with no strap: half a mile is eight hundred and eighty yards, twelve minutes, two hundred and six counts over the baseline, two set-downs at twenty-nine each; a whole mile is four hundred and twenty, which is twice two hundred and six and eight more, **and she has not worked out what the eight is.** She decides three things and then a fourth: **she does not tell anybody in that ground she is going, and if anybody asks she says east, which is a lie, and she is going to have to say it four times in three days.** And at the ninth hour of the twenty-seventh she finds that **three of the ten people at the frame are not waiting for her** — a man of forty doing two sums and a woman of thirty-four checking his working — **and that is what a figure is: it is not mine and it is not a secret, and there are three of them doing it now who were not doing it nine days ago.** She does not stop them.
- **ch 700, days 2,171–2,173, the twenty-eighth to the thirtieth, *Going West Past The Wall*.** **She goes west out of that ground on purpose for the first time since the first of the month beginning 2,084.** Four miles of ground, two miles of flat with nothing to lie down on, the wall at the seventh hour, thirteen miles of store road, the ninth mile and the post at the end of the paid road, and the bench. **The woman of sixty tells her the thing nobody at that turn could tell her: you will be there at the first hour or you will not, and the light comes up on that frame last.** At the two posts at the fifth hour of the afternoon of the thirtieth: **the slate has been wiped and put back with her name on it and thirty days beside it and nothing taken off that she can see.** And then the reversal: **he told her the fifth comes off the days and does not say so on the board, and he was right, and she had not thought to ask him what it would look like** — no mark, no column for it, no figure different from any other month's slate. **She has been able to check everything she has ever had against something, and for the first time in her life there is nothing to check this one against, and that is on purpose.**
- **ch 701, day 2,174, the first, *The Line She Had Not Seen*. REVELATION.** She is at the frame at the first hour with the light coming up on it last, six feet off, and the man of forty-nine takes the slate out and **turns it over** — and the other side, which has faced the wall for nineteen years and which nobody in this county has ever read together with the chalk side, has **a figure under every name and a fourth column of names.** **The figures are days, and every name, every month, has one in twenty of the days that name has had in its column, added to what it had already had.** Her name is four lines from the bottom and the figure under it is **ninety-eight days, which is four days and twenty-one hours and thirty-six minutes** — **and the figure she said out loud to two hundred people seventeen days before had accumulated to exactly that.** So: **every man of forty-nine in this county can work it out, every month of his working life, and does not know he is doing it.** And the fourth column is a name that came off that board; there are **three hundred and one** of them out of nine hundred and twelve, very nearly a third, **and the fourth one from the bottom is the woman of sixty sitting on a bench four hundred yards away.**
- **ch 702, days 2,175–2,205, the second of the month beginning 2,174 to the first of the month beginning 2,204, *The Room That Expected Somebody*.** Thirty days in a tower four miles up a mountain, **the longest she has been in one place since she was nine years old**, copying nine sheets a day and reading four hundred volumes and **sitting thirty feet from a locked drawer for thirty days and not using it.** She went up for one reason: **she is looking for a place where there is something to receive it, because an announcement is made to arrive and it arrives where there is something to receive it.** The boy of sixteen tells her the man who came out of that door is nine years dead. **The fifth first comes at 2,204 and she sits on the top step in the dark and counts it going past — the first first of a month she has ever let go by without wanting to be at it.** Seventy-eight days and not seventy. **IMMORTAL VESSEL 2 at the second hour of the third of the month beginning 2,204, and its cost, named once: SHE CANNOT BE MET WHERE SHE IS EXPECTED.** And then she puts her eye back to the grille and **the lamp at the far end of the long table is burning**, and the dust on the bar is not broken and the nine feet behind it has not been walked on. **"You lit a lamp."**
- **ch 703, days 2,206–2,212, the third to the ninth, *No Way Of Knowing Where To Come*.** Seven days of nothing happening again, with the lamp burning every one of them. **The arithmetic of it, not the lamp: a carter comes up that hill once a month on his own time; if she is not in the building he comes and there is no one and he takes it back down and has nowhere to take it.** His board by the door is days and not names and not one person comes and reads it. **Two records that do not meet, and nothing in this county that joins them.** **So: if I am not here on the day he comes, then on the day he comes I am nowhere. That is not a figure. That is the absence of a place.** She is going to leave the lamp burning with the boy of sixteen and says out loud, on the way down four flights, that **a lamp is the nearest thing to a person being met that this world has offered her, and she is nineteen and cannot do better than a lamp.**
- **ch 704, days 2,213–2,219, the tenth to the sixteenth of the month beginning 2,204, *What Two Hundred People Can Do*. THE VOLUME'S HARDEST CHAPTER.** She walks east for a day and a half and **does not stop at the bench**, and she says why in the first line. There is a queue at the frame and **nine of them are doing it without her**, and on the sixteenth there are fourteen, and forty people in that ground can count to eighty. **There is no craft in it: a total is a date, a day and a fraction, and the fraction is one in twenty on every ground in this county.** She thought that forty-five days ago. **She does not go and tell them, and she has three reasons and two of them are good and the third is that she cannot stop counting** — *I am not going to tell them, and I am not going to stop counting, and I am not going to be able to tell anybody which of those two is the reason.* **Not resolved.** Three hundred and sixty-four lines on the board.
- **ch 705, days 2,227–2,240, the twenty-fourth of the month beginning 2,204 to the seventh of the month beginning 2,234, *Still On This Side Of The Line*.** She leaves a queue of nine because she wanted to stand somewhere for one hour of a month where there is nothing to be useful for, **and it is the first thing she has wanted in about a year.** Three hours from the north end of that ground to the wall, six hours with the ground first, four set-downs, and she stays. **A person at a wall is the only kind of person she has ever been in this county that nobody comes and asks anything of**, and after two days she has counted nine hundred things and **being in a place has cost her nothing at all for the first time in nine years.** She goes through every single thing she has ever been the only one of knowing: **five of them, four about boards, and the fifth a lamp at the far end of a long table.** **The sixth first goes past at the first hour of day 2,234 with her standing at a wall two days of road from a frame**, and she knows what the pencil figure under her own name says that morning. On the next day a woman of thirty-four walks out of the east along the flat with a sum she could not make go, and she does it in a count of twenty, **and says the one thing she decided at the ninth hour of day 2,219 she would say to one person: there are fourteen of you now.** **She does not go and tell the fourteen anything, she has not stopped counting, and the seventh first is twenty-four days on from here.**

### VOLUME 12 BATCH 0003 — REVIEW REPAIR, one paragraph

The batch's own review audited ch 691–705 against its fifteen cards, against `outline/volume-12.md` and against every ordinal and
figure on the page, and **cleared nineteen defects across twelve chapter files, nine of them duplicated section headings and two of
them the same arithmetic error repeated in a later month.** A month-begin that was thirty days out at ch 695 · **the total not
accumulating at the rate the chapter set for it, twice — four days nine hours thirty-six minutes at day 2,164 and seven days
twenty-one hours thirty-six minutes at day 2,234, where the page had four days and eight hours thirty-six minutes both
times** · the lighter figure two days short at ch 695 · *twenty hours away* for a first hour that is eight hours off · *forty
days* for a mountain she reaches in three · **five wrong intervals in ch 704, all measured off an arrival that had moved** ·
ch 705's wall three days out of its own arrival and a fortnight for twenty-one days · ch 676, ch 695 and ch 702 all dating
ch 665's asking to a day that is not one, **which ch 665 itself contradicts and which is the one repair made outside this
batch** · ch 699's yards, its grand sum and a set-down that could not be eight counts · ch 701's bottom column contradicting
its own hero number forty words on · a tower-to-turn distance the outline forbids in any card · *the first two days of the
fourth*. **The open map question was adjudicated without being settled: the batch's own twenty miles, in twelve sentences across
nine chapters, was withdrawn because it is corroborated nowhere, ch 700's fourth statement was corrected, and the leg is now
measured the way the batch measures everything else, in days — two days of road, from ch 700's own timing.** One retired phrase
was cut to bring the batch inside its own line. **Nothing structural moved: no card beat, no day, no stage, no title, no
irreversible step, and ch 697's is the batch's only one.** Full record, the day table and the craft measurements in
`state/continuity.md` under ***VOLUME 12 BATCH 0003 — REVIEW REPAIR***.

## VOLUME 12 BATCH 0004 — ch 706–720, *what is written on it*, the last batch of Volume 12. Day 2,241 to day 2,300. Fifteen chapters, **28,680** words of body prose on method B, average 1,913. **Volume 12 as a whole: 116,766 over sixty chapters at average 1,946.** *Corrected in place by the Volume 12 close: 116,754 was the pre-repair page and is struck; corrected again by the Volume 12 close review repair, which took four words off ch 670 — 116,766 is the live volume figure and 116,770 is struck.*

**Two firsts fall in this band and not three: 2,264 and 2,294. The volume's payoff figure is written at ch 717 and derived on the page from the two dates and the fraction; no card before it states the figure and none previews it.**

- **ch 706, days 2,241–2,245, the eighth to the twelfth, *Back Under The Two Posts*.** Thirteen miles back the way she learned the road, four set-downs, the last at thirty-four counts, and the ninth-mile post at the eighth hour of the eighth. **The slate has been wiped and put back with her name in a county hand and thirty days of the month beginning 2,234 in beside it**, and there is nothing on that board she can check a figure against, and she had known that the last time and knew it twice. She stood nine feet off a bench for a count of twenty and walked back — the third time in nine years. **She counts the board at the north end of the rows by subtraction: 114 nights, four a night, four hundred and fifty-six**, with a stray stroke at the top she leaves. And she worked out at the ninth hour why she had walked two days of road to get back to a piece of slate, and said it out loud standing in the grit: she has four firsts in front of her and one of them is the last she will get for nothing. **She decides to spend it.**
- **ch 707, days 2,246–2,249, the thirteenth to the sixteenth, *The Firsts Are Only Dates*.** Four days at the frame with one question asked out loud each day. **Can the first of a month do anything to her that thirty ordinary days have not already done.** The finding, in a count of nine: a first is the day the slate is taken out, wiped, turned over, written in, put back and carried up a road at the second hour. **A column has been losing a twentieth of a day since the ninth hour of the twenty-fourth of the month beginning 2,054 and it does not stop on one.** She goes through the six firsts that have fallen since her name went on that slate, out loud, stood at two of them and watched the other four from a distance, and not one of them did anything to her the twenty-eighth of a month had not also done. **And the one thing that has never happened at one: anything arrived. Nine announcements into a county with nobody in it.** So a first is a date, not a place, and she has been standing at dates for nine years because she did not have anywhere else to stand. At the ninth hour on the sixteenth she says the thing she wants out loud to a slate, and the last of it is that she has a first in fifteen days that she has been standing at in her own head for nine years.
- **ch 708, days 2,250–2,253, the seventeenth to the twentieth, *A Record Belongs To Whoever Keeps It*.** He comes east at the ninth mile and she has one question ready and lets him get to the rail. **Is a thing written in her own hand and sent up a stair still hers.** He says **no**; a record belongs to whoever keeps it; a line does not come back, not because anyone took it, because that is not the shape of a thing that comes back; and he has kept one for nine years and has never once been able to take a line out of it. **It is the only question anybody has ever asked him that he would rather had been asked earlier**, and the first person who asked him something about a line instead of about a person is somebody she will not meet. **He says nothing about his paper, his order or his brother, she does not raise his request although she had the count of sixty after his answer to use it, and neither of them says anything else.** And she works out the consequence standing in the grit: there is no version of this in which she changes her mind on the day. **I have got to have it before the twenty-fourth and I have not got one word of it and I am going to get one.**
- **ch 709, days 2,254–2,257, the twenty-first to the twenty-fourth, *What Has To Be On A Board*.** Three conditions, chalked on a frame rail: **a date, a place, and true statements about a person in a hand** — found by taking apart the four sentences and the date she already wrote on a board four carts go past a day. **The third one has to be not her.** A name is a person and a twentieth of every day since can come off a name; a figure is not a person and there is no one to come off it. She tries the strip and it cannot go: a thing has to be pickable and walkable, and there is no machinery in this county for nine inches of paper. **A board goes up on a first and there is not one other date it will travel on, and the firsts are thirty days apart and nobody alive decides them — which is the whole reason the eighth first is thirty days on from the seventh and not sooner.** The man of forty-nine comes at the sixth hour of the twenty-fourth with the rag and the chalk and writes four columns and her name. **She decides not to write it today: a line on a board is not a decision, it stops being a decision the second it is down, and she would rather find out on the twenty-eighth of the month beginning 2,264 than on the first of the month beginning 2,294.**
- **ch 710, days 2,258–2,261, the twenty-fifth to the twenty-eighth, *The Fourth Time Past The Bench*.** The woman of sixty answers the question from ch 709 in the morning of the twenty-fifth: yes, she was on that board for four years and her name came off it and there was not one person to tell and the man with the chalk has not said anything about it in five years. **And Ning Yan does not tell her**, and works out three reasons and none of them survives being said out loud, and carries the fourth name from the bottom of the fourth column for four days. Three hundred and one names out of nine hundred and twelve is very nearly a third and she does not know what a third of a name is. She counts the posts, walks five miles west to a mill with a wheel that has not turned in nine years and stops in a yard at the cottage at the stopped lime bed on the way, walks nine miles out of thirteen to the post at the end of the paid road, and stays at the frame. **It does not get lighter and it does not get heavier and it does not come out in a number she can put against something else.** She walks past the bench for the fourth time in nine years and says nothing, at four miles an hour, lighter by seven hundred and one days. And she says the thing that costs, lying in the wet: in thirty-three days she is going to put one true thing about herself where any person in this county can read it, and four hundred yards from where she does it there is a woman who has got a thing about herself that she knows and cannot and is not going to be able to put anywhere for her.
- **ch 711, days 2,262–2,266, the twenty-ninth to the third of the month beginning 2,264, *The Seventh First Goes By Empty*. MIDPOINT.** She stands in the grit from the eleventh hour of the twenty-ninth until the light comes up on the frame, and it comes up last. **The seventh first, at the first hour, and the man of forty-nine is already there, and she is four feet back in the grit where a cart can go past without touching her.** He wipes, turns it over, and she has the side that has faced a wall for nineteen years inside the time it takes a man to turn round twice. **The figure under her name is one hundred and eighty-eight days and it is right**, and 188 out of twenty is nine days, nine hours and thirty-six minutes, **and she does not say one word of it out loud to anybody.** She watches a man who does not know he is writing a levy write four more lines of one, and there is not one person she is entitled to tell. The cart comes at the second hour; her name goes up a road with thirty days in a county hand and a fifth comes off the days exactly as a fifth always has, and there is no mark anywhere on the board that says so. **And nothing else goes up.** She wrote nothing, and the reason she gave at the ninth hour is: *I do not have a line yet, and a line is not a number I have not said, it is a thing I mean.* She wants to write something. She works out the cycle of a board. **Five hundred and fifty-two strokes at the north end of a ground two days of road east while she stood in a gravel turn doing nothing, and not one of the four men can read the line that is hers.** And then she asks the woman of sixty the only question she has: *did you ever have a first of a month with nothing on it.* **Nine of them about, and the same every time, and that is the whole of what a first of a month is.** And the woman of sixty says: *you are going to be standing here on the first of the next month after next and I am going to be on this bench, and you are not going to be standing here for nothing.*
- **ch 712, days 2,267–2,270, the fourth to the seventh, *Public Property On A Stick*.** Two days of road east and **five hundred and sixty-four strokes on a board she is not at**, and about forty feet off it a woman of thirty-four with a slate on her knees and a man of forty behind her, not waiting for anybody. **Thirteen people at the frame doing sums and not waiting for her; forty who can count to eighty; a boy of seventeen getting his carts wrong on purpose.** She works out what the frame has become: about a third of a man's working life is checked by somebody who is not him, and the man is still right about everything. **And then the third reason changes and she says so out loud at the south end in daylight: it is not any of her three reasons, it is that the thing she was protecting them from has a date on it — in twenty-five days there is going to be a line in her own hand with her name and her figure on it at a frame two days of road west, and any one of the fourteen can walk there and read it.** And she decides **not to tell them**, and gives one reason for it and it is a reason she would not put in front of anybody: **she wants it to be a fact in a county and not a thing a woman said, and she is going to have to live with not knowing which of the thirteen gets there before she does.**
- **ch 713, days 2,271–2,274, the eighth to the eleventh, *He Worked His Out Five Years Ago*. REVERSAL.** Ning Wu in the third row, forty feet in, the hands of their father, nineteen, carrying stones for four men for nine years, and he has not said her name to her since a road five miles west of a mill nine years ago and he did not say it now. She asks one question — **how many** — and he asks why, and she tells him instead that she has worked out her own total and said it out loud in front of two hundred people and cannot be un-said. **He puts the stone down and says *five years*, and then: *I have carried four thousand stones for nine years and you do not get to carry them and not know*.** And they say the same sentence at the same time from opposite ends of a row of stones: **there is not one person to give it to.** She stays four days; she carries stones for an hour; she learns that the fourth man has been short three days running and one man has said so and that is the whole of the system's reporting. He says a thing on a board in a county is worse than a thing said at a frame, **because a person can be asked and a county does not get asked.** She does not answer him. **Nothing is settled, he does not call her anything, she does not call him anything, there are two people in the county who know a figure about themselves and neither of them wants the other's.** Five hundred and eighty-four strokes on a board and twenty days to go.
- **ch 714, days 2,275–2,279, the twelfth to the sixteenth, *An Old Man And A Shut Book*.** Four miles up, nine days of copy and two of reading, a locked drawer thirty feet away with the key in her bag that she does not use, and a boy of sixteen who says **it is all right, I have never opened it, I do not know what is in it and I am not going to.** She asks about the oil: **enough until the weather turns, and after that nothing in the building, and the carter brings it once a month and has never once asked who the lamp is for.** She says the whole of her position out loud in the ordinary voice. She reads the third shelf out of four hundred volumes and asks whether anything has ever come up that stair: **four bundles in thirty-one years, boards, and a man at the top of it who has been there thirty-one years and puts the door bar back across the outside.** Eight flights, nine counts of breath at the top, her eye at the grille, the bar's dust not broken anywhere, **and she does not lift it.** And an old man with a shut book under his arm goes up the mountain road past the yard and does not stop and does not answer her question and **is not going to the tower, because the road goes on past it** — alive, old, and it was not to her and there was not one thing about it she was owed. She goes down four miles and says out loud that she is going to be at a frame at the first hour on the first of the month beginning 2,294 and **two days after that standing in that yard again**, and that a boy of sixteen is the only person in this county who has been told.
- **ch 715, days 2,280–2,281, the seventeenth and the eighteenth, *The Count She Wrote Herself*. REVELATION.** The question she took down the mountain with, put the same way three days running: **how is she going to be counted at a first of a month.** And the first answer is one she has known since she was ten — **a count takes a board and not a person**, and she has never in her life been counted where she stands, and everything that has ever come off her came off it in a place she was not in, from a hand that was not hers, on a day she did not choose. **And then, on the store road, something has been settled and it comes out of the ground under her feet and not out of her hands** — not off two coppers, not off an arm, not off the place behind her fourth rib, not off four counts of breath — **and she decides not to count it, the first thing in nineteen years she has decided not to count.** She works out the shape of the line and says it out loud four times in four days to a road. And the finding that ends the chapter: **the only thing that can be counted at a first of a month is a line, and the count she cannot escape is the one she wrote myself** — a name on a slate she asked for on the twenty-fourth of the month beginning 2,054, four sentences on a board on the fifteenth of the month beginning 2,114 that a carter read a name off three days later, and a line in thirteen days. Every one of those three was her and nobody made her do any of them.
- **ch 716, days 2,284–2,290, the twenty-first to the twenty-seventh, *A Count And Not A Person*. IMMORTAL VESSEL 3 AND ITS COST.** Two days of road east to a building with the shutters up and a hundred people reading behind her, and she asks a man of sixty-five with a book whether it sits on the road a line takes. **It does not.** A slate at a turn goes up a road to the seat once a month in a bundle with about forty other boards, and four people at the seat have never looked at one, and then a bundle goes up a stair in a tower four miles up a mountain on the arm of a man who has carried four of them in thirty-one years, and on each of the four a man came out of that door and took his hat off, and it comes down empty. **2,290 − 2,206 = 84, and the six on top of seventy-eight is because there is a date on this one and the date has to be a first.** She reads the sheet on the wall every day of the four — ninety-eight thousand against ninety-five thousand and eighty, a difference of two thousand nine hundred and twenty that is attributed to nothing and is not the levy — and does not say it out loud. **At the second hour of the twenty-seventh, standing at the end of the counter, it takes the length of a breath and there is no announcement and a hundred people do not look up.** And he tells her the price once, in the ordinary voice, and does not soften it: **you cannot be counted where she stands. The count is a count of boards. She is not on the board, and there is not one thing in this county that can put her in one, and the harvest takes a board and has never taken a person.** And she says the finding and not the cost, out loud, at the counter: **a room will read a line and it will not know a woman wrote it, and she is going to write it anyway.**
- **ch 717, days 2,291–2,294, the twenty-eighth to the first, *In Her Own Hand And Only Once*. THE PAYOFF AND THE HAND.** The whole of the thirtieth of the month beginning 2,264 and she writes nothing. **Two thousand two hundred and ninety-four less two thousand and seventy-seven is two hundred and seventeen, and counted with the first day as the first it is two hundred and eighteen days; two hundred and eighteen out of twenty is ten and nine tenths of a day; nine tenths of a day is twenty-one hours and thirty-six minutes.** At the first hour of the eighth first she is four feet back in the grit with three people inside a hundred yards and a fourth four hundred yards west, all counted before she takes anything out of the bag. **She does not lift the nib** — a nib laid on cold slate and not lifted takes, and one that is lifted and put back does not, and that is why one is never written twice — **and she writes with her right hand on an arm that was open, outdoors, in daylight, in the grit: *Ning Yan, the two posts. Two hundred and eighteen days. One in twenty. Ten days, twenty-one hours, thirty-six minutes.*** **Nineteen words**, and the same sum under it in figures and again in words. **The little finger of her right hand stopped being part of her hand somewhere in the middle of that line.** Three fingers and a thumb, and the arm did not close afterwards and there is no closing it, and she picked the bar up in a count of twenty-nine, which is the part she found unbearable. The cart comes at the second hour and the board goes up west. And then **there is a sound in the air at that frame for about nine seconds** — not loud, not inside her head and not outside it, and there for all four of them — **and it is never printed in the book**, and she has tried to put it down and cannot, and she is not going to try again, and **there are four people within four hundred yards of that frame who were not asked for it and not one of the four will say the same thing about it. She is not going to be one of the four.** And the chapter does not close on her standing still: it closes on her working out, in daylight, standing in the grit, that the line is going to go up a stair into a room and be read by somebody she will never meet and cannot find out about, **and that the four of them are going to go on saying four different things about the same nine seconds for the rest of their lives and not one of the four will ever be able to correct the other three.**
- **ch 718, days 2,295–2,296, the second to the third, *Four Accounts That Do Not Agree*. THE HARVEST AND THE BATCH'S IRREVERSIBLE STEP.** Two days at the frame and the accounts come back into the turn without her asking. **A man of forty-nine who will not say what he heard and says he has not got it right yet and has four columns to write · a woman of sixty who says it was a number · a carter of about fifty who says it was nothing, that he was three hundred yards off and heard his own axle, and that his nine-year-old son heard it · a boy of nine who says it was me.** Four accounts, no two of them agreeing, none repeatable, three of the four declining to try. She writes all four on a frame rail with chalk so she cannot lose them. **And then the harvest: the levy is one in twenty of every day a name has had in a column, it has been coming off her since the ninth hour of the twenty-fourth of the month beginning 2,054, and it is not something that happens to a board — she wrote a figure the levy had already taken, so it has come off the writing and it has not come off her again. The harvest took a board and did not take a person, and there is no instrument in this county that knows how to take a person off a piece of wood, and there is no instrument in the world that takes a line out of a book that has already gone up a stair.** **And the bill for Court is settled**: the thing that reads records did not read the board at the north end of the rows, it wanted a shape with a hand on it, and it has one, and a room at the top of a stair four miles up a mountain has read it, and **she did not agree to it and she says out loud she would have written it anyway and she is not going to be able to write a line that does not have her name at the end of it ever again.** She goes west at the fourth hour of the third.
- **ch 719, days 2,297–2,299, the fourth to the sixth, *Nothing On The Other Side Of The Grille*.** Thirteen miles of store road and four miles up on an arm that has been open since the morning she wrote the line, four set-downs, **the last at fifty-four counts, which is more than it has ever cost her and four times what it cost a fortnight ago.** Eight flights and eight hundred and eighty steps with a bar nine feet nine: the shoulder goes at about the three hundredth step of the first four, she picks the bar up twice on the second four at twenty-nine counts each, and she does not think about anything at all on any of it. The short breath is at the third landing of the second four and goes to nine and she does not sit down. **And she says out loud that there is a bundle going up that stair four days old that she cannot see, with a figure in it in her own hand, about six days higher up than she is.** She asks the boy about the oil: the weather has turned and there is about a day and a half in the jar, **and he says he will keep it going after she stops asking.** She puts the bar down at the bottom of them on the way and the fifty-four is a set-down that is not a set at a door, because she is not going to set it at a door. **The dust on the bar across the low door is not broken anywhere and the nine feet behind it has not been walked on.** Through the grille: a long table, a lamp in a cage at the far end, a stub of a candle in a dish, no chairs. **She says out loud that she cannot describe what is in that room and is not going to try, and that she is not met and she is expected, and that she is not going to say that sentence again.** And then she decides: not tonight, and not with an empty hand and nothing to have put on a table, and **she will leave the bag at the bottom of the first four and come up these eight flights at the first hour tomorrow morning with an empty hand and she is not going to lift the bar either, and she is going to stand on the outside of it and find out what is on the other side in the light.**
- **ch 720, day 2,300, the seventh of the month beginning 2,294, *One Step Up A Second Stair*. THE LAST PAGE OF VOLUME 12.** Eight flights and eight hundred and eighty steps with a bar nine feet nine, the hardest thing she has done since a stage past a ceiling that no longer exists, and a boy of sixteen at the bottom of the first four who does not come up. **The low door is standing open the width of a person, and the bar is on the floor inside the room against the wall on the left, with the dust broken at one end and not at the other, and there is no mark on the landing and no mark on the rail.** She stands on the landing and looks at an open door for a count of forty and does not go through it, and then goes through it. **A long table down the middle, a lamp in a cage burning at the far end, and that is all of it.** She works out the whole of what has happened to her out loud in the room, standing, in daylight: **what she said was ten days, twenty-one hours and thirty-six minutes, and it is in a book now that does not need her to be alive to be right. She is nineteen. She has three fingers and a thumb, an arm that is open and is not going to close, four counts of breath and seven on anything that rises, a place behind her fourth rib the size of a coin, seven hundred and forty days, five blank leaves against her ribs that she is not going to write in, a key in the front of her bag for a drawer she is not going to open, two coppers she cannot count, and a figure in a book that somebody read before they wrote anything.** She left the bag at the bottom of the stair; the four sheets are in a locked drawer four miles behind her and she has not looked at the margin since the twenty-third of the month beginning 2,024 and is not going to. **And so she went up eight flights with nothing to give anybody in her hands but the bar she has carried since she was nine years old, and she has been able to be given nothing her whole life and has not understood until this morning that that is the same thing.** And then: **there is nothing in that room she can put a figure to, and she has not in nineteen years been in a place she could not put a figure to, and she is not going to describe it and not going to write one word of it in five blank leaves and not going to say it out loud to a stair. She went up one step, and she stopped there.**

---

## VOLUME 12 — *The Road Up*, ch 661–720 — THE VOLUME IN ONE COMPACT PASS (Volume 12 close)

**Sixty chapters, day 2,061 to day 2,300, 116,766 words of body prose on method B at average 1,946 — 29,996 · 28,850 · 29,240 · 28,680 by batch — zero bracketed layer lines in sixty, eight firsts at 2,084 · 2,114 · 2,144 · 2,174 · 2,204 · 2,234 · 2,264 · 2,294, Immortal Vessel 1 · 2 · 3 at 2,128 · 2,206 · 2,290.** In this volume Ning Yan must ascend at all, or the designation is harvested and everything Yuan spent is spent for nothing — and she does, carrying it on paper instead of on herself, paying a finger, an open arm, and every line she will ever write again.

**Batch 0001, *the stair* (ch 661–675): she asks whether a person can be announced without being designated (ch 665, un-askable), climbs the only *up* on the page, and puts her name on a board for the first time in eleven volumes (ch 674, irreversible).** Batch 0002, *the lamps* (ch 676–690): the Order returns with a budget — a total with a period and one of its lines hers from four volumes ago — and the 2,920 is attributed to nothing; he takes Court and becomes findable (ch 687); she gives him the pen in daylight with six terms (ch 689, irreversible, the volume's real damage); Immortal Vessel 1 with the cost *she cannot be given a shape*, named once. **Batch 0003, *the firsts* (ch 691–705): the slate goes east blank and comes back readable; she says her own total out loud in front of two hundred people (ch 697, irreversible — eighty-one days, four days and one hour and twelve minutes); the slate turned over shows every name's figure and a fourth column of 301 out of 912; Immortal Vessel 2 with the cost *she cannot be met where she is expected*, named once; the sixth first goes past with her at a wall.** Batch 0004, *what is written on it* (ch 706–720): the seventh first goes by empty with 188 days under her name; her brother works out his own figure and tells nobody (ch 713); Immortal Vessel 3 with the cost *she cannot be counted where she stands*, said once in another mouth (ch 716); nineteen true words in her own hand on the eighth first with the little finger stopping mid-line (ch 717); nine seconds of sound and four disagreeing accounts with the harvest taking a board and not a person (ch 718, irreversible); the low door open, the long table, the lamp, the second stair, and one step up it (ch 720).

**What changed that could not have changed before it:** she is on boards and in a book above her; a figure about a person can now be said out loud and checked; the announcement went up instead of into the ground; being heard is what the levy was for and she has paid for it; three fingers and a thumb; one step up a second stair. **Next: Volume 13 planning, `workspace/volume-13-planning/PROMPT.md`.**

## VOLUME 13 BATCH 0001 — ch 721–735, *the bundles*, the opening batch of Volume 13. Day 2,301 to day 2,360. Fifteen chapters, **29,923** words of body prose on method B (every line not beginning `#`, counted per file with `grep -v '^#' <file> | wc -w` and summed), average 1,994. Per chapter: 721 2,043 · 722 1,946 · 723 1,786 · 724 1,970 · 725 1,787 · 726 1,987 · 727 1,928 · 728 1,923 · 729 1,916 · 730 1,818 · 731 1,865 · 732 2,042 · 733 2,305 · 734 2,112 · 735 2,495. Zero bracketed layer lines in fifteen. **CORRECTED AT THE VOLUME 13 BATCH 0002 REVIEW REPAIR: this block carried 29,691 at average 1,979 and its own per-chapter list summed to 29,684, both measured before commit `8a93acd` edited nine of these files. Re-measured on the committed page it is 29,923 and 1,994. 29,691 and 29,684 are the pre-repair page and must not be quoted.**

**Two firsts fall in this band and not three: 2,324 and 2,354. The three dated days this batch cannot move are all in it: the first bundle at day 2,324, the second at day 2,354, and the day spent at day 2,357.**

- **ch 721, days 2,301–2,304, the eighth to the eleventh, *The Dust On The Bar*.** She comes down off the second step into a room with a person in it, and the person did not appear in any of the nine times she counted that room through the grille, because the lamp is on a shelf at the far end and the light goes away from the door and she has been counting the near end. She watches for four hundred counts and the hands on the far edge of the table do not move once. **She says out loud, standing, in daylight, in the ordinary voice, the whole of what she is, one item at a time, and nothing in the room takes it.** She wants a person to look at her, says that out loud once and says she is not going to say it again to that table. She names the broken dust on the bar once and says she is not going to work out what it means, and says that nothing from this room goes in five blank leaves, standing, in daylight, and does not improve on it. **She decides she is not going up the second stair today and that she is going to find out what decides in this room first.**
- **ch 722, days 2,305–2,308, the twelfth to the fifteenth, *Nothing In It She Can Count*.** Four days counting the room. Eight things: a table, a lamp in a cage, a stub of candle in a dish, a door, a bar, a stair, a person, a floor. The floor is nine feet by a shade over six; the table is twenty-two paces long and four feet high. **She tries to put the person at the table into a number and cannot**, and says so: she has counted two hundred people in a ground and a column behind a piece of wood that faces a wall and never a person standing in the room with her. The door has no mark on it and there is no light in the room but the lamp. **She works out, out loud, that nobody above this line loses a day, so days are not counted above the line, and she is still losing them at one hour and twelve minutes a day and cannot see the day turn.** She wants a second thing in the room that changes while she is in it.
- **ch 723, days 2,309–2,312, the sixteenth to the nineteenth, *Nine Hundred Steps*.** **The second stair goes up and comes back down to the floor of the room at the far end, out of a place in the wall behind the lamp's shelf, which is why seven steps are visible from the door.** Counting a stair that turns is counting turns; sixty-four turns, nine long runs, one hundred and forty steps in the longest. **At nine hundred the number stops being a thing she can hold**, and she says so out loud on a stair, and puts it nowhere, and the five blank leaves stay blank. She cannot sleep there, she says, and she is going anyway. **She decides she will have to go down eight flights for the nib and the ink and is not going to do it this month**, because she is not going to write down a number she has not finished counting.
- **ch 724, days 2,313–2,316, the twentieth to the twenty-third, *The Second Place At The Table*.** Four days, four tries. The edge of that table is a hand's width above the top of her reach; on the second day she puts her shoulder into the frame and the table moves a quarter of an inch on the stone and every count of breath goes out of her at once; on the third she hangs on with three fingers and a thumb and it comes out of her hand; on the fourth she gets a hand on the edge and holds it for a count of nine. She gets up on it and goes along it on her knees and looks along the length of it. **On the far side there is a stretch the length of her forearm where the wood is paler and smoother and has a shape worn into it that is not a mark anybody made with a tool, and nothing is in front of it and nothing is on it.** She does not touch it, does not name it, and **does not say one word about it out loud.** She says out loud only that there are two places at that table and one of them is being used.
- **ch 725, days 2,317–2,320, the twenty-fourth to the twenty-seventh, *No Oil In The Building*.** She goes twenty-two paces along the top of the table on her hands and knees with no rail and her breath at nine. **There is no dish, no bowl, no reservoir, no wick, no key, no door in the side of it and nothing on the shelf it stands on, and there is wall behind the light.** **The flame does not lean when she moves her hand past it sixty times in a room with a door standing open.** There is a short line cut in the stone of the wall behind the lamp and the bottom of the light is a hand's breadth and a little over it. And there is a boy of sixteen four miles down this mountain whose day and a half of oil went out on the third of this month, who said he would keep it going after she stopped asking, and **she does not say one word of that out loud.** She says out loud that the lamp is not fed out of a jar and that there is a person at that table and that is all she is going to say about how it is lit.
- **ch 726, days 2,321–2,324, the twenty-eighth to the first, *What Came Up The Stair*.** Twenty-three days of waiting, counted out loud every day, and the finding that **a first up here is not a date, it is a day something comes up a stair.** At the second hour of the first of the month beginning 2,324 she hears boots on the second stair, on the turn, and a man of about forty comes into the light with about forty boards under one arm and a bundle of papers in a cloth under the other, puts them on the table in one movement and goes back out without looking at anything. **The hand goes along them at four to a minute and does not stop: forty boards is ten minutes, and the bundle of papers is the other thirty.** She gets a hand on the near edge before they go on and then lets it go, and **does not touch the table once in forty minutes**. And from four feet off she cannot tell her own board from the other thirty-nine, which is the first time in nine years that a thing she has made is indistinguishable from a thing she has not made.
- **ch 727, days 2,325–2,328, the second to the fifth, *Forty Boards And Not One Of Them Hers*.** She goes under the table head first, four times in four days, with a cross-beam across her back and her breath at nine and no light at all. **She cannot read a board in the dark and she can read a board with her fingers**, because she knows her own hand. On the eleventh board there is a mark, and on the twelfth, and on the thirteenth, and it is the same on all three: one stroke in the same corner and one figure of the same shape, and by the fifth she has had her hand on all forty and **not one of them is different from the others and not one of them has anything else on it.** **A room does not read boards: a figure that comes out the same on forty different boards has not come out of what is on the boards.** She says out loud that she has been counting the wrong thing for a year, and he says that is the first thing she has said up here that she did not know when she said it, and she says it is the first thing she has said up here at all that he has heard.
- **ch 728, days 2,329–2,332, the sixth to the ninth, *One Look And No Second*. MIDPOINT.** She redoes the forty minutes herself: nine times at four to a minute, a stroke, a figure, and one look of the same length every time, and she lies on the table with her cheek on the wood at the height that head is at and cannot tell one board from another. **The deciding takes one look. There is no second look and no third and nothing to appeal with and nowhere to stand and wait for one.** **The look did not include her**: she was four feet off the end of that table on the first and was not one of the things that went along it, and **nobody has refused her and nobody has spoken to her.** A thing that arrives with no row is not a thing that has arrived, and there is no procedure for a thing that has not arrived, and there is not one because it has never happened. **And then she says the last part out loud, alone, at the near end of the table with her back to the light: the not writing was not a decision anybody made.** There is a hand that looks at things at four to a minute and a figure that is the same on every one and a look that is one look, and not one of the three knows she exists. **She says what she wants: a row.** And she works out that a thing that has not arrived cannot be made to arrive by a person waiting for it.
- **ch 729, days 2,333–2,336, the tenth to the thirteenth, *A Figure She Cannot Hold*.** The row is off the table and under it, flat on stone, face up, a foot long and two inches across, and it was not there on the first. She reads it with her fingers lying on her side with the cross-beam across her back, three times. **A head, a column, one thing in the column, and the one thing is ten days, twenty-one hours and thirty-six minutes in her own hand.** She works out why: a figure copied any other way than it was written is not that figure, and this room has been putting figures in a column for four hundred years and every one has to come out exactly right. **So the only thing this building has ever copied perfectly, in four hundred years, is the price of her.** There is no name and no place and no date and nothing on it about what she is. **The figure is true as of the first of the month beginning 2,294 and it is going: on the twelfth of the month beginning 2,324 it is forty-one days out, which is two thousand nine hundred and fifty-two minutes, which is forty-nine hours and twelve minutes, which is two days and one hour and twelve minutes.** **There is not one person in that building who could ever know that figure is going**, because there is not one person above this line who loses a day. And she cannot hold it, because it is not hers to hold.
- **ch 730, days 2,337–2,340, the fourteenth to the seventeenth, *A Rate She Can Measure*.** She puts her hand flat, edge on, against the wall behind the lamp and measures the bottom edge of the light with it: nothing on the fourteenth, half a hand on the fifteenth, a whole hand on the sixteenth, half again on the seventeenth. **Two of her own hand a day.** She measures the top of the cage to the cut in the wall in four goes and gets fifty-eight. She writes nothing down and holds four numbers. **At the fifth hour of the seventeenth the figure under the table is two days and seven hours and twelve minutes out of date**, forty-six days of an hour and twelve minutes by subtraction off 2,294. **And she cannot find one sentence for the two of them.** She has said everything she has found since the eighth of this month out loud and this is the first thing she has had to hold back, and the reason is that she has two things going down and no way of knowing one is why the other is, and she will not say a guess in a room where a hand copies figures exactly. **Nobody above this line has an instrument for it.**
- **ch 731, days 2,341–2,344, the eighteenth to the twenty-first, *Left Against The Wall*.** She picks the bar up three times on purpose to take the figures: twenty-nine counts to lift it, fifty-four to set it down, and fifty-four every time. **A bar is not furniture and a bar is not a thing you own; it is a thing for putting a load on something that is not flat and the whole of it is the shoulder, and there is nothing in this room for a bar to be put down for.** She works out that carrying it up nine hundred and four steps at nine counts of breath standing still with an open arm and three fingers and a thumb is not a thing she can do and be standing on that table at the end of it. **On the twenty-first she puts it down flat against the wall on the left for the last time, broken end towards the door, and does not look at the dust on the top of it, and says out loud to the bar and not to a person: I am leaving you here.** She has never once in nineteen years put anything of hers down in a room on purpose. **And she can go down those eight hundred and eighty steps with nothing in her hands for the first time since she was nine.**
- **ch 732, days 2,345–2,348, the twenty-second to the twenty-fifth, *Twenty-Nine Days To The Mark*.** She cannot count it between two turnings because she has seen one, and because the room counts in hands and she counts in days and a hand turns a lamp as often as it has to and no hand has ever written down how often that is. **Fifty-eight divided by two is twenty-nine, done four times standing on the floor with her feet on the stone.** Twenty-nine days from the first of a month is the thirtieth, and the next turning is on the first of the next, and **so there is one day in every month in which that lamp stands on the line with nothing above it and nothing below it, and there has not been one of those days used for four hundred years.** She works out that the mark from the feed on the first of this month is on the thirtieth, which is 2,353, **eight days from the twenty-second**. She says out loud that she is not going to tell the room, because the room is not late and a hand turns the lamp as often as it has to, and because the only thing it would produce is a room with a reason to look at her. **And she cannot add two counts in different kinds of thing.**
- **ch 733, days 2,349–2,352, the twenty-sixth to the twenty-ninth, *The Same Substance*. CLIMAX.** At the second hour of the twenty-eighth, two days before her own sum says the mark is reached, **a hand comes off the near edge of that table for the first time in twenty-two days and goes up into the cage.** The light comes down to the line cut in the wall and stops on it, and then the hand opens and the light goes up off the cut, past the top of the cage, higher than it has ever been. **The whole of it is eleven seconds and she counts them.** Afterwards the forearm on the table's edge is smaller by the width of the little finger on her left hand, and the face is thinner at the cheek, and the person does not stop and does not look up. **She measures both: the round that came off that forearm and the distance the light came up that wall are the same.** She does not know what it is and says so, and she can compute one turning as the width of a little finger across a forearm and can compute her own to the hour, and **has no figure at all for how many turnings there have been, because a hand turns a lamp as often as it has to.** She says a version of the same thing has been coming off her every day since she was nine. And she tells him, in the room and not out loud, that she could do it and is not going to, and the only reason she has is that she does not know what it is.
- **ch 734, days 2,353–2,356, the thirtieth to the third, *What A Hand Is Charged*.** The second bundle comes at the second hour of the first of the month beginning 2,354 and she stands four feet off the end of the table for forty minutes again and does not put a hand on it once. **The light is six of her own hand below the top of the cage, which is three days of falling, and the last turning was three days ago and not on the thirtieth, so twenty-nine is not the number.** She reads the strip under the table with her fingers and it is the same figure. She watches the second turning and measures the arm before and after and does not need to count out loud this time: the same width, the same seconds, and not one movement a person standing at that table would have looked up for. She puts the whole of it out loud, one item at a time. **And the one part that is a person: the face at that table is fuller than it was on the first of the month beginning 2,294, and there is no month in it at all, two turnings in twenty-seven days and then three days and none in between, and there have been four hundred years of it.** She works out that **a room cannot be asked, it can be paid**, and the price is that somebody has to stand at the table and it comes off that person. **She decides she is not going down eight hundred and eighty steps for a nib and an ink, and that she is going to be in this room on the thirtieth of this month, and she does not know what for yet.**
- **ch 735, days 2,357–2,360, the fourth to the seventh, *One Day Into A Lamp*. IRREVERSIBLE — THE BATCH'S ONLY ONE.** She counts out loud everything she has and none of it is a day. **Everything that has ever come off her has come off her in a place she was not in, from a hand that was not hers, on a day she did not choose, and she has never spent one hour of one day out of her own life by her own hand.** He tells her she is not going to be able to feel it go, and she says then she will do the sum out loud afterwards. **At the fourth hour of the fourth of the month beginning 2,354 she gets onto the table, goes twenty-two paces on her hands and knees, puts her hand up between four bars of iron and closes it on the base of the light.** The light goes up, past the top of the cage, higher than it has ever been, and stays there when she takes her hand out. **Two thousand three hundred and fifty-seven less one thousand five hundred and sixty is seven hundred and ninety-seven, and it is seven hundred and ninety-eight now, and that is two days in one day.** **From today the figure she is lighter by is the day less one thousand five hundred and fifty-nine**, and she says that is a day a day and has been one every day since the eighteenth of the month beginning 1,544. Twenty-six days of light had been left in that lamp and now there are twenty-seven, and the mark is on the first of the month beginning 2,384, which is the day the next bundle comes. **That is the first day of slack in the month that has never once been spent in four hundred years, and she has spent it, and there is not one row in that building with her name at the head of it that has anything in it about what she did.** Three days after, the light is ten hands below the top of the cage and fifty-eight less ten is forty-eight and forty-eight divided by two is twenty-four and the twenty-fourth day from the seventh of the month beginning 2,354 is the first of the month beginning 2,384. **She does not do it again, because she has no instrument for what came out.** The person at the table does not turn round, nothing is marked anywhere, the strip under the table still carries the same figure and is three days, three hours and thirty-six minutes out of date, and **she has been in that room sixty days and none of them has gone into the book.** She ends on what she cannot find out: what the lamp is paying for, and there is not one thing in that room that is going to tell her.

**What changed that could not have changed before it:** there is a person in the room and it is not looking at her · she can reach a table and she cannot reach a thing that is on it · there is a stair above her that comes back down to the room she is standing in · the lamp has no oil, no wick and nothing behind it, and it does not lean in a draught · a bundle comes up the stair on the first and forty boards go along a table and the room does not read boards · the deciding takes one look and it did not include her · there is a row on the floor under the table with her figure at the head of it in her own hand and it is going stale · the light comes down two hands a day against a cut in a wall and the mark is twenty-nine days out, which puts one day a month back · a hand and the light are the same substance · **she has spent a day, and the figure she is lighter by is `D − 1,559` from today and there is no instrument anywhere that takes it back.**

## VOLUME 13 BATCH 0002 — ch 736–750, *the row*, the second batch of Volume 13. Day 2,361 to day 2,420. Fifteen chapters, **30,152** words of body prose on method B (every line not beginning `#`, counted per file with `grep -v '^#' <file> | wc -w` and summed), average 2,010. Per chapter: 736 2,061 · 737 1,843 · 738 1,941 · 739 1,861 · 740 1,985 · 741 1,949 · 742 2,133 · 743 1,855 · 744 1,980 · 745 2,167 · 746 2,347 · 747 1,980 · 748 2,254 · 749 1,999 · 750 1,797. Zero bracketed layer lines in fifteen. **Re-measured at the batch's review repair; the first draft measured 29,988 at average 1,999 and the difference is the two boy-of-sixteen lines added at ch 746 and ch 747. Batch 0001 is 29,923, so the volume is 60,075 over thirty chapters at average 2,002.**

**Two firsts fall in this band and not three: 2,384 and 2,414. The three dated days the batch cannot move are all in it: the third bundle at 2,384, the stage at 2,403, and the fourth bundle at 2,414 — and the name is said at 2,411, the tenth hour, because the batch card's own day table and its own prose date contradicted each other and the day table is the authority.** **The name is said on the twenty-eighth of the month beginning 2,384 and ch 750 now recalls it on that day at both places; the eighth of a month would be day 2,421, after the chapter ends.** Full record, the day table, the three moving series and the craft measurements in `state/continuity.md` under ***VOLUME 13 BATCH 0002 — ch 736–750, \*the row\****, and every repair under ***VOLUME 13 BATCH 0002 — REVIEW REPAIR***.

- **ch 736, days 2,361–2,364, the eighth to the eleventh, *The Question He Carried Up*.** **THE BOY ARRIVES.** Boots on the second stair at the sixth hour, slow, with a stop on the turn, and she says out loud that it is not the man who comes up on the first. **A boy of sixteen, wet through, holding the doorframe, who has come up eight hundred and eighty steps in the dark with no rail on the top four hundred and forty, because the lamp on the fourth landing went out on the third of the month beginning two thousand two hundred and ninety-four and there has been no oil in the building for nine years.** He asks one question: why is the lamp at the top of this tower still lit when the lamp he was told to keep going is not. **She cannot answer it and she says so, standing, in daylight, in the ordinary voice, and it is the first time in nineteen years a person has come to her with a question and she has had nothing for it.** She gives him three true things that are not answers. **She wanted to put something in his hand and she has two coppers she cannot count and nothing else.** He goes down and she listens to him go. And then she works out that there is not one lamp on that stair and there has not been one for nine years, and that she has been going up and down it in the dark for sixty days and has never once thought about what it is, **and that she is going to have to take him back down it herself and has nothing to say to him on the way.**
- **ch 737, days 2,365–2,368, the twelfth to the fifteenth, *Four Hundred And Forty With No Rail*.** She takes him down the second four and watches, having asked him nothing. **He gets a hundred and ninety steps down in an hour and has to sit, and then gets up and does them.** On the thirteenth she takes him down the whole of that half and it takes a count of nine hundred. On the fourteenth she counts him down all **eight hundred and eighty**, first four with her hand on the rail and the second four with the wall four inches off and nothing on the other side, and she says it out loud. **And he puts both hands on the edge of that four-foot table and gets up on it in one movement and she cannot, and she puts her own hand up to show him and gets about a hand's width of palm on the wood and cannot get her weight onto it, and says that is the whole of what is different about her and about him.** He asks what the table is for and she says **I do not know**, and it is seventy days and she has counted everything else in the room. **And then the truest thing she has about that place: nothing in this room sees anybody standing on it. She has been on it twice and he stood on the middle of it for a count of nine and the person at the table did not look up, and she did not know that until he did it.**
- **ch 738, days 2,369–2,372, the sixteenth to the nineteenth, *Four Strips Under A Table*.** She goes under the table and **he cannot follow her, which is the first thing she is better at than him at in seventy days**, and she says so out loud. **There are four rows under that table**: a foot long and two inches across, face up, square-edged, every one with a head at one end that is a figure and every one with a column. **She cannot read a figure on the three old ones and works out after four days why: not one stroke in any column on any of them has a turn in it, and the width of the cut is the same on every mark, and on hers it is not, because she holds a nib with three fingers and a thumb. So the column is a column of figures and not a list of things.** **Three of them have gone soft at every edge and hers is two months old and the cuts in it still catch; she can say they are older by a great deal and cannot say how much and is not going to guess.** What she wants, said out loud before she decided to say it, is a name on one of the three — and if there is not one, then a thing gets into a row without anybody standing in the room saying it. And she cannot read a strip by touch and neither can he, and he has copied figures off a board since he was fourteen and never had to do that. She ends on the thing she cannot do: **I have got four of them under a table and not one word on any of them that came out of this room, and I do not know who put it in the row and I do not know what puts a thing in one.**
- **ch 739, days 2,373–2,376, the twentieth to the twenty-third, *The Hand Does Not Enter Anybody*. REVERSAL.** She goes along the top of the table on her hands and knees and puts her face level with the wood and looks at it from six inches for a count of three hundred. **There is nothing on that table to write with** — no ink, no stone, no sand, no pen, no holder, no knife, no rag, no water, no blotter, on four feet of pale wood. He asks the plain question and she says it is the whole of what she has got and she has not got an answer to it. Then she lies on the floor and does it again from the beginning in order, out loud: **her figure came out of her row in her own hand with the strokes not all the same width, and a hand that copies exactly and has nothing to write with did not make it — so the hand cannot enter anybody into anything, it can only enter a thing somebody has already said.** **And then the reversal of the reversal: the look at ch 728 is not deciding whether to write. A thing that has arrived has already been decided on somewhere else, in a room somebody stood in, out loud, standing up, and it came here to be copied.** The room does not read. The room stands in the room where the thing happened. And she says out loud that this is a second wrong instrument and it is worse than the first, because the first was a thing she had the wrong end of and this one is a thing she did not know she was looking at. **The boy asks what she is going to do with the figures. She has been putting them where they are and keeping them, and that is the whole of what she has ever done with any of them.**
- **ch 740, days 2,377–2,380, the twenty-fourth to the twenty-seventh, *A Row She Could Say Out Loud*. DECISION.** **She has been able to say a thing out loud, standing up, in daylight, in the ordinary voice, since she was nine years old, and there has never once been a hand in the room that copies figures exactly.** Four days laying the four strips out and working out what a row wants: a head at one end that is a figure, a column that holds figures and not things, and a size of one person saying one thing. **She has got the head and she has got nothing for the column, and those are two different problems and one of them is finished.** She works out that the figure she has been carrying since the ninth hour of the twenty-fourth of the month beginning two thousand and fifty-four is on three things in her life and the figure at the head of her own row, **and that it is what she is worth and not what she is.** She says what she wants out loud: to stand up in a room and say a thing about herself and have it go somewhere. She says the thing she was at nine — four capital strokes on a leaf out of a blanket because a thing in capitals is a thing that cannot be got at — and sits on the floor and says nothing for a count of a hundred and forty. **And she does not do it, because a column is not a price, and because the first thing ever said in that room about her is going to be said with a boy of sixteen standing in it, and she cannot choose it.**
- **ch 741, days 2,381–2,384, the twenty-eighth to the first, *The Third Forty Boards*.** The third bundle at the second hour, about forty boards and a bundle of papers, put down in one movement and gone. **The boy counts them going on and gets nine and then starts counting the time, and she tells him to count them going off, and he gets forty and works out ten minutes on his own and says *How did you know that before I started*, and she says because she counted it twice and did not get one of them wrong.** **There is a second person who can count. That is all. She has been the only one in every room for nineteen years and it has taken nineteen years to find the second one and he is sixteen.** In the thirty minutes the bundle takes she watches him look at a leaf of paper going along the table and be entirely unable to make anything of it, and she tells him that somebody has to stand in a room and say it first, which is the other half of what she worked out at ch 739. **And then he asks what she does with the number, and she finds out that she has not got an answer, and that it is the first question anybody has stood in a room and asked her in nineteen years, and she is nineteen and he is sixteen and does not know what he has done.** At the fourth hour **the light comes down onto the line cut in the stone and stops on it with nothing above it and nothing below it**, which is the first day in four hundred years that has happened and the day she bought at ch 735, **and there is not one row in that building with a word about it.**
- **ch 742, days 2,385–2,388, the second to the fifth, *Counting Him Down Eight Hundred And Eighty*. REVERSAL.** **A hand goes up into the cage at the second hour of the second of that month — the third turning she has watched — and she counts the seconds and does not need to say the number out loud and then does, and the forearm is smaller again by the width of the little finger on her left hand and the face is thinner at the cheek, and nothing about the standing has changed.** She tells him he has to go down and he says **All right** and she says that is not a thing to say all right to.
