She takes him down at the second hour of the third and **counts all of it out loud, four hundred and forty with the rail and four hundred and forty without, sitting down at three hundred and eighty for a count of twenty with him on the step above her and not saying anything.** She walks past the bag at the bottom of the first four, names every object in it out loud, **and says out loud that she decided on the third of the month beginning two thousand three hundred and fifty-four not to go down for any of them, has not in nine days, is not going to today, and has not changed her mind** — and says the drawer is a different decision and she is not going to run them together in front of him. And she counts them down all eight hundred and eighty and gives him the number instead of an answer and knows it is not an answer. At the bottom he asks how she knew and she says because she counted it twice, and **he asks what a lamp at the top of a stair does with nothing to put in it and she tells him the truth, which is that she does not know, and he goes and puts his hand out to the lamp on that landing and takes it back.**
- **ch 743, days 2,389–2,392, the sixth to the ninth, *What The Room Stands On*. MIDPOINT.** The room with one person missing in it, and she says out loud that she had a second counter for four days and has not got one now. **MIDPOINT: a row is entered by the person it is about and not by the hand at the table, the hand writes what the person says and nothing else, and the head of it is a figure.** Worked out of the furniture in six days: **there is no dust on the top of her strip and there is a handprint standing in the dust on the top of the second one from the seventeenth of the month beginning two thousand three hundred and fifty-four, and that hand has never once been near a row in a hundred and twenty days.** A hand at a table cannot enter anybody into anything, because there is nothing on that table to write with. Her figure came out in her own hand and she chose it at two posts on a road before it was on a board. **And who keeps it: the room stands on it.** The table is for things that arrive and the row is for things that are about people, and the row is on the floor and the table is four feet above it and nothing goes between them. **And the rule is four hundred years old and there is not one mark in that building that says why, and nobody in it remembers making it, and the reason it exists is the reason she needs it — a hand cannot put a person into a row, so nobody there can decide what a person is — and she is never going to be able to ask, because the only way to ask a question in that building is to put it in a row, and she has not got one.**
- **ch 744, days 2,393–2,396, the tenth to the thirteenth, *One Figure Said Out Loud*. DECISION.** She chooses the day, the hour, the place and the posture, and says all four out loud on the morning before, so that nothing is left over for her to do differently at the time. **At the tenth hour of the tenth of that month she says eight hundred and thirty-four.** Nothing else — no date, no place, nothing about what the figure is the price of. **The hands come off the near edge and go along under the shelf and a board comes out and a stroke goes on it and a figure goes on it and the board goes on the stack, and that person does not pause and does not look up and does not do one single thing different from what they did with forty boards.** She says out loud that the hand does not know that was hers, and that it is not deciding, it is copying. **And she works out that she lost the only instrument she had: for ninety days she was a thing that had arrived with no row and she could go and stand where the table was and see that nothing had been written about her, and now she cannot check what is in a row at all.** She does not read the board. She gives the reason out loud — if it is her figure then the room has her price and not her, and if it is not then she has put a thing she cannot check into a room with one look and no second, and either way she cannot act on it, **and she has never in nineteen years held a figure she could act on.** She wanted it to be her. **The board goes up that stair on the first of the month beginning two thousand four hundred and fourteen and comes back down empty, and she is not going to find out what is in it for a very long time, and she said it anyway.**
- **ch 745, days 2,397–2,400, the fourteenth to the seventeenth, *The Arm That Wrote It Down*. REVERSAL.** Six readings of that forearm and the sixth is down by the width of the little finger on her left hand. **Forty boards in ten minutes and a bundle in thirty cost that arm nothing; one figure copied exactly cost the same as one lamp.** She did not take the sixth reading on the day because she was watching the writing, and she says out loud that that is the only thing in ninety-nine days she has got wrong that she could have got right. **The saying is free and the writing is not, and the cost comes off the hand that writes and not off the person it is about.** Three charges in eleven days she can see and four hundred years she cannot, and she has no figure and is not going to make one up. And then the human sentence, said out loud, alone: **somebody stood at that table for four hundred years and put their arm into a lamp for words other people said out loud in rooms they were not in, and they never found out whose words they were, and they never once thought about it.** She thinks about the boy four miles down that mountain, who can count and does not know what a row is and is not going to be told by her. **And the reversal: it does not cost her anything. She can say a row as many times as she has days for and every one of them costs that arm a little and nobody will stop her and nobody will be angry, and that is the worst thing anybody has ever handed her, and it was worked out in four days off a forearm, by her.**
- **ch 746, days 2,401–2,404, the eighteenth to the twenty-first, *Off The Table Herself*. IRREVERSIBLE — IMMORTAL VESSEL 4 AND ITS COST, NAMED ONCE.** Two days working out how a stage is taken above the line: **there is no board, no slate, no counter and no ground; a stage is taken by being written in a row, by a hand, in the light of that lamp, on a day the feeder chooses, and the feeder is a hand that goes into the cage.** She gets a board out of the stack under that table herself, backwards, on her back, with the cross-beam across her shoulders. **At the tenth hour of the twentieth she gets up on the near end of the table, goes twenty-two paces along the top of it on her hands and knees with her breath at nine, kneels on the shelf in front of the lamp and writes a figure in her own hand with three fingers and a thumb on an arm that has been open since the first of the month beginning two thousand two hundred and ninety-four. Four. Immortal Vessel 4.** No announcement, no sound, no tribulation, and the person at the table did not look up. **2,206 − 2,128 = 78 and 2,290 − 2,206 = 84 and 2,403 − 2,290 = 113, said out loud: a stage past the third working point is taken against a row and there was no row to take it against, and it is a hundred and thirteen and not seventy-eight.** **THE COST, NAMED ONCE: she cannot be given a thing** — and she says out loud that the fourteen carried costs are still true and are not able to be put down, and that this one is not the same sentence as any of them, because this one says the way through is now the only way there is. **Seven of eleven holds where it was eight, and she could not work out where the day went off her and did not guess in a room where a hand copies figures exactly.** And a board in a record that nobody in that building knows is hers. **And the boy of sixteen, at the end, in what he cannot do: he can get up on that table in one movement and he cannot get under it, and there is not one thing in that building that would have put a hand under it for him, and she went under it on her back with a beam across her shoulders and it took her two tries to get one board out of that stack of forty.**
- **ch 747, days 2,405–2,408, the twenty-second to the twenty-fifth, *What A Row Has To Say*. DECISION.** She says her whole inventory out loud, standing, in daylight, in a room with a person in it: nineteen years old, three fingers and a thumb, an arm that is open and is the arm she writes with, four counts of breath and seven on a rise and nine standing still, **seven of eleven holds**, a small open place behind her fourth rib the size of a coin, **eight hundred and forty-nine days lighter at one hour and twelve minutes a day**, not hungry, four stages into the seventh realm. **And then she stops for two days.** A row has to say what a thing is, and a figure is what a thing costs, and the price is not a row, the price is the head of a row. There are two things she could put in a column that are not a price: **a name, which is not free and does not stop and takes a fifth of a day out of a column every day**; **and a sentence, which is what a column is not for and a hand that copies figures exactly will not put in one and hand back.** And there is a third thing. **There is a voice, and it has exactly one name in it, and it is not hers, and it is on the third leaf of a book of eight leaves made out of a blanket that has been against her ribs for ten years in four capital strokes in her own hand, and she wrote it when she was nine because a thing in capitals is a thing that cannot be got at.** And then the finding that made her sit down: **the six terms do not survive this room.** They survive two people who said them out loud standing up in daylight in a ground with stones in it. A hand is not one of the two. There is no figure in the world that means in daylight or standing up, and if she says them in that room they are four sentences about nothing. She stands with her back against the bar and works out the shape of what she has to say and does not say it, and says out loud that she is not going to say it on the twenty-sixth, or the twenty-seventh, or the twenty-eighth, or the twenty-ninth, **and that this is the first time in nineteen years she has said out loud that she does not know a day and meant it.** **She has got six days to the first of the month beginning two thousand four hundred and fourteen and the sums do not change that. The boy of sixteen, in what he cannot do: he came up eight hundred and eighty steps to ask her one question and she gave him a number, and he is the only person on either side of that line who could say out loud that he heard her say the two words, and a hand that copies figures exactly is not going to take his word for one word of it.**
- **ch 748, days 2,409–2,412, the twenty-sixth to the twenty-ninth, *Two Words In An Ordinary Voice*. IRREVERSIBLE — THE BATCH'S.** **The one at the table goes out of that room four times in four days and she is under the table every one of them**, and she does not follow and does not stand in the doorway, and she names the cost she has carried without saying it out loud in nineteen years: **a room that is expecting somebody cannot be met by her.** At the tenth hour of the twenty-eighth she says the whole of what she is, standing, in daylight, in the ordinary voice, one item at a time, in front of a person at a table. **In the ten seconds before the two words he asks her not to. One question, in the ordinary voice, standing, in daylight, and it is the first time in nineteen years that anything in her head has spoken to her while there was a person in the room: *Is there a way to do this that does not have me in it.* She does not answer it. She says `Yuan Sheng` anyway.** The hands come off the near edge for the first time in a hundred and twenty days with nothing on the table; a board comes out of the stack and is not the top one; a stroke goes on it and something else goes on it. **He does not ask a second time and he does not hide it.** He says what it is and what it costs in the ordinary voice, standing, in daylight: a name is easier to find than a shape, a mouth is the only thing that carries a name anywhere, and **the thing that reads records does not speak when it finds something.** Then **she says the six terms out loud in that room, one at a time, standing, in daylight, as she has said them in every room she has ever said them in, and there was not a seventh one and she did not ask for one.** She did it because a row has to say what a thing is and what she is includes the voice and the six terms do not survive being read to a fourth person. **She did not do it for him. He did not ask her twice.**
- **ch 749, days 2,413–2,416, the thirtieth to the third, *Nothing Speaks To Him*. REVERSAL.** **The boy comes up that stair a third time** and says he has not come to ask her again and she says he has come to get an answer he can carry down, and he says the man counting the boards at the far end of that floor has started asking instead. **On the first of the month beginning two thousand four hundred and fourteen the fourth bundle comes up the stair with a name in it**, ten minutes of boards and thirty of papers, four to a minute, not stopping, and she cannot tell which of the forty the board she got written on is. **And on that day the light comes down onto the line cut in the stone again and stands there with nothing above it and nothing below it.** She waits two full days for something and nothing comes: no mark on that table, no stroke, no figure anywhere, no hand off the near edge except for the boards. **He is found, and nothing speaks to him, and she is the only person in the world who knows what is in that row and she cannot see it.** She does not tell the boy and says why in one line. **And he stands in the doorway for a count of ninety and looks along that table the way a person looks at a room and notices the place on the far side where the wood is not like the rest of it and there is no chair, and she says I know, and does not say one word about it out loud, in daylight, in that room, and tells him not to touch the table.** She has had her arm into the bundle of papers under the far end three times in about a hundred and fifteen days and **has not pulled it once**, and says why: because it is the only thing in that room that has not been looked at, and a cord round it twice and not knotted is a thing a hand goes straight into, and a room that has had four hundred years to shut a thing is not a room that shuts things.
- **ch 750, days 2,417–2,420, the fourth to the seventh, *The One Thing She Cannot Work Out*. DECISION.** She takes him down **eight hundred and eighty a second time** and counts it out loud again and says she is not going to count it a third time, and at the bottom he says *You have got it by heart* and she says because you count it, **and it is the first time in nineteen years that there has been a second one anywhere, and I am not going to be the one who stops.** Then she counts what is in that room that is about her: **three things** — the strip under the table with her price at the head of it, **six days, three hours and thirty-six minutes out of date** · the board with her own stage figure in her own hand, gone up on the first of the month beginning two thousand four hundred and fourteen and come back empty · and the board with his name in it, **said out loud on the twenty-eighth of the month beginning two thousand three hundred and eighty-four** and gone up on the same bundle — **and not one other thing in the building knows about any of the three.** And two coppers she cannot count, a bar against that wall she put there on purpose, four fingers and a thumb, five blank leaves blank for ten years, a bag she is not going down for. **And then the light: it was turned at the second hour of the second of the month beginning two thousand three hundred and eighty-four, it comes down two of her own hand a day, and it was four of her own hand below the top of that cage on the fourth of this month, six on the fifth, eight on the sixth and ten on the seventh; she does the sum on the fifth, fifty-eight less six is fifty-two and fifty-two divided by two is twenty-six, and the twenty-sixth day from the fifth of this month is the first of the month beginning two thousand four hundred and forty-four, which is the day the next forty things come up that stair.** **She works out that she could put a day into that cage and push the line, and do it again and again, and hold that lamp off the line for a year out of eight hundred and sixty-one days of her own. And she is not going to** — not because it is dangerous, but because she has no instrument for what came out, and because she has a row in that room and does not know what it is for. And the chapter ends on the one thing she has been there a hundred and nineteen days to find out: **what a row that has her in it is for. And she is not going to find out by asking, and she is not going to find out by waiting either, because the light comes down two of her own hand a day whether she knows what a row is for or not.**

**What changed that could not have changed before it:** a boy of sixteen is in a room where one look decides everything, and she is the smallest thing in it and he can reach the table and she cannot · **there are four rows under that table, three of them four hundred years old, and she is in one** · **a row is entered by the person it is about and not by the hand at the table, and the room stands on it, and nobody above the line remembers making that rule and nobody ever will** · **she has said a figure out loud in that room and a hand wrote it on a board that went up the stair on the first** · **she is four stages into the seventh realm and she cannot be given a thing, and she took it off the table herself in front of a room that has never watched anybody do that** · **a row costs the hand that writes, not the person it is about, and there is no limit on it and nobody will be angry** · **`Yuan Sheng` is in a record above the line and he asked her not to once and she did it anyway and he did not ask twice** · **the thing that reads records has found him and does not speak** · **and she has a row in the room with her in it and cannot work out what it is for.**

## VOLUME 13 BATCH 0003 — ch 751–765, *the measure*, the third batch of Volume 13. Day 2,421 to day 2,480. Fifteen chapters, **31,000** words of body prose on method B (every line not beginning `#`, counted per file with `grep -v '^#' <file> | wc -w` and summed), average 2,067. Per chapter: 751 1,989 · 752 2,132 · 753 1,850 · 754 2,131 · 755 2,202 · 756 1,981 · 757 2,034 · 758 2,098 · 759 2,138 · 760 2,157 · 761 1,994 · 762 2,088 · 763 2,129 · 764 1,951 · 765 2,126. Zero bracketed layer lines in fifteen. Batch 0001 is 29,923 and Batch 0002 is 30,152, so the volume is **91,075 over forty-five chapters at average 2,024.** Two firsts fall in this band: **2,444 and 2,474.** The stage is at **2,461** and the batch's irreversible step is at **ch 763.** Full record, the day table, the four moving series and the craft measurements in `state/continuity.md` under ***VOLUME 13 BATCH 0003 — ch 751–765, \*the measure\****, and the repair that produced these figures in ***VOLUME 13 BATCH 0003 — REVIEW REPAIR***. **These are the final post-repair figures: the drafting pass measured 30,920 at average 2,061 and a volume of 91,080, all three wrong by arithmetic and all three struck; the Volume 13 close then measured 30,988 and corrected ch 762 by twelve words; and this figure is the one the page actually carries. Nothing in the plot, the day table, the four series, the stage, the frame scene or the irreversible step moved in either repair.**

- **ch 751, days 2,421–2,424, the eighth to the eleventh, *Two Things Standing Still*. DISCOVERY.** She stands at the length the arms in that room go for four days — up on the table at the near end, twenty-two paces, kneeling on the shelf in front of that lamp, sitting in the light with her arm open across her knees — and **nothing in that room looks at her once**, and the person at the far end of that table does not move. Then she says the plainest sentence of the volume: **I wanted to be measured.** She says there is not one instrument in this building that measures anything, and that there is no rule and no mark with a number on it and nothing to put a thing against. **She goes under the table and finds the answer: a row has two ends, the head is a figure and the column is figures, and every figure at the head of every row in that building was said out loud by a person in some other room and came up a stair in an armful, so it has not been measured, it has been moved.** The only thing it can be measured against is a person, and that person has not changed in four hundred years. **And she cannot get angry about it, because the one at that table has never once decided anything.** The change: she has been a figure at the head of a column for a hundred and twenty-three days and **not one person in this building has ever held a rule up beside her**, and what he counts is worth nothing here.
- **ch 752, days 2,425–2,428, the twelfth to the fifteenth, *Nib And Ink Against The Fourth Rib*. DECISION.** She goes down **eight hundred and eighty** steps in the dark, four hundred and forty with a rail and four hundred and forty without, and past the cold iron of the lamp in the cage on the fourth landing that a boy of sixteen has been keeping going with nothing in it since the third of the month beginning 2,294. **At the bottom she names all nine things in the bag out loud, picks up two of them — a stub of a nib and a cake of ink opened on the seventh of the month beginning 1,874 and never let dry — and puts them inside her shirt against the fourth rib, and says out loud that the decision of the third of the month beginning 2,354 was never about the nib or the ink, it was about the drawer, and that she is doing one of those decisions and not taking either of them back.** She goes back up with nothing in either hand and no rail on the lower four hundred and forty. **Then on the fifteenth she goes down the second stair with both hands empty, counts the sixty-four turns she can hold, and stops at the sixty-fourth and says out loud that she cannot get to the bottom of that and come back in what she has left, and that she is not going down it for anything until she can do it in one push, and that she is not going to try it in one push until she knows how long it is.** The chapter's last line is the drawer and the four sheets of rice paper in it, and not the nib.
- **ch 753, days 2,429–2,432, the sixteenth to the nineteenth, *A Mark On A First*. DISCOVERY.** Fifty-eight of her own hand, two a day, twenty-nine days, and a month is thirty — done four times and it does not get a different answer for being done a fifth time. **Twenty-nine days from a turning is a thirtieth when the turning is on a first and a first when the turning is on a second, and the last turning was at the second hour of the second of the month beginning 2,414, and the reason it was the second is the day she put into that cage on the fourth of the month beginning 2,354.** So the mark falls on **the first of the month beginning 2,444** and not on a thirtieth, for the second time in four hundred years, and there is not one row in that building with a word about it. **And the reversal she did not intend: she bought one day with a day and what she actually bought was the loss of the one day of slack that every month in four hundred years has had.** She says it out loud twice because she wants to hear it in her own mouth, and she does not tell the room, and the man who brought forty things up that stair did not look at the wall behind the lamp on his way out.
- **ch 754, days 2,433–2,436, the twentieth to the twenty-third, *The Four She Had Not Counted*. REVELATION.** She refuses to put the number on one of the forty boards, because there is nothing above the line that counts steps, and says out loud that saying a figure out loud in a room with a person in it is a place a figure can go and it is the only place she has. She changes the way she holds it: turns out loud, runs out loud, and **a hand on the wall at every hundred.** **At the second hour of the twenty-third she goes down the whole way with nothing in her hands and comes back up the whole way, and the count is nine hundred and four.** Four short sentences carry the revelation: *The whole of it is nine hundred and four* · *I counted to nine hundred and I could not go on* · *The four I could not hold were the last four* · *They are the last four steps of that stair.* And then the one that is the point of the chapter: **this is the first distance she has ever counted in nineteen years, and the first figure she has had to put anywhere other than in her head, and she put it in a room, out loud.** She says at the end that she is going up it again before the first of that month with nothing in her hands.
- **ch 755, days 2,437–2,440, the twenty-fourth to the twenty-seventh, *A Number Is Not A Wall*. DISCOVERY.** She counts the second stair upward out of the floor of the room, which she has never once done, and finds that **the four steps she could not hold are the first four out of that room, not the last four**, and that on the nineteenth of the month beginning 2,294 what stopped her was not that she had run out of stair. Then she stands at the foot of it in daylight and works out the rest: **there is no nine hundred and fifth. That stair has two ends and both of them are in this room, and one of them is under her feet.** And the plainest account she gives of her own time in a hundred and thirty-eight days: **she was using the number as a reason.** Not being able to hold a figure is not the same as there being something in the way, and she had been standing at the top of nine hundred for five months telling herself she had no place to put it, and she had known how to say a figure out loud since she was six.
- **ch 756, days 2,441–2,445, the twenty-eighth to the second, *The Fifth Bundle And The Cut*. REVERSAL.** She waits through the twenty-eighth, the twenty-ninth and the thirtieth with the light fifty-two, fifty-four and fifty-six of her own hand below the top of that cage. **On the first of the month beginning 2,444 the fifth bundle comes up the stair at the second hour, forty boards in ten minutes and a bundle of papers in thirty, and at the fourth hour the light comes down onto the cut in the stone and stops on it for the second time in four hundred years.** She says the whole of it out loud in a room with a person in it and **nobody in that room hears it and she had known that before she said it.** At the second hour of the second, a hand goes up into that cage and the light comes up past the top in about eleven seconds and that arm is smaller by the width of the little finger on her left hand. **And she cannot tell what it cost that arm: it is the width of one figure, and it could be the figure she said on the tenth of the month beginning 2,384, it could be the name she said on the twenty-eighth of that month, and it could be neither, because forty things go up that stair on the first of a month and one hand copies every one of them exactly and has never once known whose words it was writing.** That is the reversal: the room can take a figure off her without her knowing which figure it was.
- **ch 757, days 2,446–2,447, the third and the fourth, *Up It With Empty Hands*. REVERSAL.** She puts the nib and the ink down on the stone on purpose and goes down the second stair with both hands empty and counts it going up: **nine hundred and four, and it is the same going up as it came down, which is the one thing about it she had not known on the nineteenth of the month beginning 2,294.** Coming back, **she loses a step at the thirty-second turn and goes down hard on her left hip and puts her open right arm into a step edge and takes the skin off it from the wrist to a third of the way up the forearm**, and she finds that she did not lose the count — seven hundred and forty-one — because the number was in the wall and not in her head. **And that is what not being able to hold a number is: not that the number goes, but that everything you know stops arriving.** She says out loud at the seventh hour that she has been at the top of that stair and at the bottom of it on the same day twice in nineteen years, and that the first time was the twenty-third of the month beginning 2,414 and it is the only instrument she has ever had, and that it is the same nine hundred and four both ways.
- **ch 758, days 2,448, the fifth, *The Place At The Far Side*. DISCOVERY — THE NINTH FRAME SCENE, AND THE FIRST IN THE MANUSCRIPT ABOUT A PLACE HE COULD GET INTO.** In her hearing first: she stands in the middle of the floor and tries to hold still the way the person at that table holds still, **and manages a count of eleven, and works out that the reason is not that the room would look at her — the room does not look at her — the reason is that being looked at is what holds a person still, and there is not one thing in this building that is looking at her.** Then the frame. **He is in the room, he has no weight, the floor does not know he is on it, and he is smaller than the table is tall and no figure of that is given.** There are two places at that table; the far one has twenty-two paces of nothing on it and then a stretch the length of a forearm, paler and smoother, **with a shape worn into it that no tool made**, and the shape is **not a hand.** He goes round it once without touching it. **It is the right size for him. It is addressed. Nobody put him in it.** He says, in the present tense, that it is a piece of furniture and that he is not going to say yes to it and that he is also not going to say no to it, because not one instruction in that room put him in it and there is not one person in that room he would be able to say no to. **And his last finding is her finding: it does not look at you.** Every sentence he speaks is in the present tense and no other character is in a marked present frame anywhere in Volume 13.
- **ch 759, days 2,449–2,452, the sixth to the ninth, *The Body At The Table*. REVELATION.** She does not re-derive the row rule. She asks the question she had not asked in a hundred and forty-five days, which is where a figure gets compared with anything, and the answer is: **a copy is not a measure, there is no number in this building for a figure to be more than or less than, and the only thing in that room a figure can be a measure of is the person standing at that table.** So every figure that has gone into a column in that room in four hundred years is a statement about that one arm, and **the figure at the head of her row is a statement about somebody else's arm and says nothing whatever about what she is.** And then the sentence it costs her: **there is a person at the far end of that table who has spent themselves keeping this county's arithmetic in order and has not been told, once, in four hundred years, that every number they have written down is a number about them.** She reads all four strips under that table with her fingers and finds that not one of the other three has a second figure in its column and not one has an empty pair of cuts, and she says one word out loud about the second place at the table for the first time in a hundred and forty-nine days.
- **ch 760, days 2,453–2,456, the tenth to the thirteenth, *Standing Still In Front Of It*. DECISION.** She stands where the room puts things, at the near end of that table, and holds still for a count of nine, and **there is no ground above the line and she cannot choose where she stands.** On the eleventh she gets up on the table, goes twenty-two paces, kneels on the shelf in front of that lamp **and says a row out loud on purpose where the arms go** — her age, her hand, the aperture behind her fourth rib, and the figure at the head of her row. At the ninth hour of the twelfth the hands come off the near edge **with nothing in front of them**, go along the far side, come down the leg of the table, come the rest of the way to the floor, **close on her open right forearm at the wrist and go its length once and hold on the wrist bone for about eleven seconds** — and **her forearm goes down by the width of the little finger on her left hand, which is the first time in four hundred years the cost of that instrument has come off anything except the person standing at the table.** Then a board comes out and a stroke and a figure go onto it, and she will not climb down and look, and she knows what the figure is: **ten days, twenty-one hours and thirty-six minutes. She said her age and it wrote down what she cost.** She says out loud that it is wrong, and that she cannot correct it because a hand that copies figures exactly cannot copy a different figure and she did not say a different figure, and that nobody in that building is going to correct it, and that the board goes up that stair on the first of the month beginning 2,474.
- **ch 761, days 2,457–2,461, the fourteenth to the eighteenth, *The Fifth Vessel*. IRREVERSIBLE — IMMORTAL VESSEL 5 AND ITS COST, NAMED ONCE.** She takes a second board out of the stack herself, goes up on the table, goes twenty-two paces, kneels on the shelf and **writes five with the nib and the ink she has been carrying against the fourth rib for seven days, in her own hand, on an open arm, with three fingers and a thumb.** Nothing happens and nobody looks up. **The interval said out loud: 2,461 − 2,403 = fifty-eight, and it is not a hundred and thirteen, and the reason is that the first three stages were taken against a room and a room is not a row, and the fourth was a hundred and thirteen because there was no row to take it against.** **THE COST, NAMED ONCE: she cannot be measured with a person.** A comparison takes two things standing still and it took one of them off her on the twelfth of that month and it takes the other one off the same person every time that lamp is turned, **and so from today the thing that does the measuring will be her and the thing being measured will be a record, and if there is a person in it, that person is furniture.** Seven of eleven holds, unchanged all day, and she does not guess why. She ends on the decision: **there is a column with one figure in it and she is going to write a second figure in it before the first of that month and it is not going to be a price.**
- **ch 762, days 2,462–2,464, the nineteenth to the twenty-first, *Under The Table With The Nib*. DECISION — AND THE CHAPTER IS THE DECISION, NOT THE DOING.** She goes under the table with the nib and the ink against her ribs and reads her own strip with her fingers, and runs her finger down the column past the figure that is in it, **and finds there are fourteen pairs of cuts in that column, one of them has her price in it, and thirteen are empty and no dust on any of them has been disturbed.** And **no second figure in any of the four strips in that room in four hundred years, and this is the first column in the building that has ever had room left in it after a figure went in.** She works out the two kinds of figure — **a figure that is a price can only be added up, a figure that is a person can be counted** — and says out loud that being counted is the thing she has been escaping since she was nine. She then goes through the three ways of being wrong and finds none of them, and says out loud that she is not going to open the locked drawer either, **because if there is a page in there with a true line on it then the last thing in the world that has never been written on is not blank any more, and she would rather be wrong in a column than right on a piece of paper nobody reads.** She decides, standing, in daylight, in the ordinary voice, and does not do it that day, and gives the reason: she took a stage on the eighteenth and does not yet know whether a column can take a second figure without taking something else out of whoever put it there.
- **ch 763, days 2,465–2,467, the twenty-second to the twenty-fourth, *Two Figures In One Column*. IRREVERSIBLE — THE BATCH'S.** She takes her own strip off the stone for the first time in one hundred and seventy-one days, **refuses a board out of the stack and says why: it is the only piece of that room that has ever been hers and she is not going to put it in a bundle with forty other things.** She says out loud, standing, in daylight, in the ordinary voice, that a figure that is a price can only be added up and a figure that is a person can be counted, **and that being counted is the thing she has been escaping since she was nine years old.** Then she writes **five**, one stroke and a turn, in the second pair of cuts down in that column, with the nib, with three fingers and a thumb, on an open arm — **and her own forearm goes down by the width of the little finger on her left hand while the nib is still on the wood, because the cost of the writing comes off the hand that writes, and the hand that wrote it was hers.** She reads both figures back with her fingers and says it plainly: **that is two figures in one column, and a column with two figures in it is a column that gets counted, because one of them is a price and one of them is not, and the only way a room can do anything with it is add them up — and it cannot be added up, because ten days, twenty-one hours and thirty-six minutes and five is not a figure.** And the last of it: **being entered is not the same as being rescued, and she has not confused the two for one minute, and she did not put her own figure in a column so that something would come and take it out.**
- **ch 764, days 2,468–2,472, the twenty-fifth to the twenty-eighth, *Two Clocks On One Piece Of Wood*. REVERSAL.** Five days of doing nothing at all to a thing she has already done, and it is new: she takes readings of her own forearm at the fifth and the seventh hour and they are the same to the width of a hair; she eats and works out that the whole of the arrangement was that there was not one person there to notice whether she did; and **she catches herself counting out loud what the room was keeping for her and stops with her mouth shut.** Then she reads both figures with her fingers every day and goes through the three older strips. And then she finds the thing she had not thought of when she did it: **the head of the row goes out of date at one hour and twelve minutes a day and five does not, and five went out of date the day after the sixth stage, and the day the sixth stage is taken is a day a hand in the light of that lamp chooses.** So in four days she has made herself uncorrectable twice: one figure is wrong by a quarter of an hour every hour and the other is wrong from the day after the next time she takes something off that table, **and there is not one instrument in the world that can put either of them right.**
- **ch 765, days 2,473–2,480, the thirtieth to the seventh, *The First Free Thing She Decided*. DECISION.** **The sixth bundle comes up the stair on the first of the month beginning 2,474** and she stands four feet off the end of that table and watches it for the sixth time and cannot tell which two of the forty carried the two things that are hers. She goes under the table and reads her own strip: **the head and the first pair of cuts both have ten days, twenty-one hours and thirty-six minutes, the second pair has five in her own hand, and twelve pairs below that are empty.** At the fourth hour the light comes down onto the cut again, the third first of a month, and a hand goes up into that cage at the second hour of the second. She does not go down the low door for five days, she puts her hand into the cold iron of the cage on the fourth landing and comes back up, and she says out loud that she is not going down for the bag, not going down for the drawer, **and not going down to tell a boy of sixteen what a row is.** And then, on the sixth, at the ninth hour, **she says his name out loud for the first time in nine years above the line: Ning Wu.** He is two days of road and six hundred feet below her, he was nine years in a barrow ground, and he has not given that up in nine years. **And she says she is not going to, and says it is not a promise and it is a figure: she has got nine hundred and twenty-one days lighter than she was and is not going to spend one of them on a road. That is the first thing she has decided in nineteen years that no mouth in this building asked her about and nothing was going to undo for her.** He settles nothing and he is not on a page of any book and the year is not named once. She ends on the four things she cannot work out before the first of the month beginning 2,504, and on not having said a single word about what she is going to do.

**What changed that could not have changed before it:** **the instrument in that room is a person, and every figure in every column in this county for four hundred years is a comparison against one arm, and not one person in that building has ever been told** · **she went down nine hundred and four steps in the dark and counted it and came back up, and the four steps she could not hold were the first four out of the room, and there is no nine hundred and fifth** · **she has a nib and ink for the first time in nineteen years** · **the mark on that wall falls on a first of a month and not a thirtieth, and that is her own doing** · **the room's instrument came off her own arm and wrote down a price when she said what she was** · **she is five stages into the seventh realm and cannot be measured with a person** · **there are two figures in one column in a piece of wood under that table and it cannot be added up** · **he has seen the second place at that table and has not got into it and has not said no to it** · **and she has said a brother's name out loud and decided she is not going back, and it is the first free decision of that kind she has made.**

### VOLUME 13 BATCH 0003 — REVIEW REPAIR, what moved and what did not

**The batch is the same fifteen chapters on the same days and the repair moved no story.** Six chapter endings were taken off a `She said,` line and onto action or silence — **ch 752** onto the lamp and the hands that do not stop, **ch 755** onto the foot of the stair and the wait for the first of the month, **ch 757** onto her standing in the middle of the floor to hold still, which hands ch 758 directly, **ch 759** onto her fingers in an empty pair of cuts and her leaving it empty, **ch 761** onto the board with her five on it going back under the shelf where she cannot see it go, and **ch 762** onto her not going down either stair. **Ch 763 and ch 765 were left on their speech endings deliberately**, the first because the reviewer called it a strong turn and the second because it is the batch's irreversible decision. Endings on a `She said,` line are now **seven of fifteen**, against thirteen before.

**`In the ordinary voice` is now one per chapter, fifteen in the batch**, each on a different beat, against seventy-one before. **The sentence *She said, out loud, on the flat of her back, in the ordinary voice:* now stands once, at ch 751, where it is introduced**; the other five instances are physical descriptions of the five places she says it from. **`She said,` is 285 words, against 293.**

**One repetition was found while repairing and cut: ch 763 said *I have been escaping being counted since I was nine years old* twice on the page**, once under *What A Price Is* and again under *What Cannot Be Undone*. The second is gone and its bar-and-wall detail folded into the sentence that replaced it. **A ch 755 speech that restated the nine hundred and four line four paragraphs after the chapter had already said it in full was also cut**, and that is where the words came from that keep the batch inside its band.

**The figures this section carries are the post-repair page: 31,000 words at an average of 2,067, and 91,075 for the volume over forty-five chapters at an average of 2,024.** The drafting figures of 30,920, 2,061 and 91,080 were wrong by arithmetic and are struck everywhere they appeared, along with *no prose of the work-in-progress was retained* — **three paragraphs of ch 751 were carried across from it** — and the standing-heading base of 757, which is 750 before the batch and 765 after. **318 open quotes and 318 close · 80 section headings · 669 paragraphs, seventeen over about ninety words and none over about 120 · `nobody` 5 · `eleven` 10 · zero brackets, bold, italics, rules, tables, fences, em dashes, straight quotes and three-digit Arabic numerals.** Full record in `state/continuity.md` under ***VOLUME 13 BATCH 0003 — REVIEW REPAIR***, and the two figures the Volume 13 close moved again — 30,988 → 31,000 and ch 762 2,076 → 2,088 — are recorded there under ***VOLUME 13 CLOSE — REVIEW REPAIR***.

## VOLUME 13 BATCH 0004 — ch 766–780, *the second look*, days 2,481 to 2,540

**Fifteen chapters, 27,876 words of body prose on method B, average 1,858. The volume is 118,951 over sixty chapters at an average of 1,983. This is the last batch of Volume 13 and it is the volume's climax.** Pressure *the second look*, which is Batch 0003's opposite and does not repeat it. Irreversible step at **ch 778**. Midpoint at **ch 772**. Climax at **ch 776 and ch 778**. *Re-measured at the Batch 0004 review repair; 27,831 and 118,894 are the pre-repair page and must not be quoted, and the Volume 13 close then moved the volume to 118,951 without moving this batch.*

- **ch 766, days 2,481–2,484, the eighth to the eleventh, *Sixteen Days Under That Table*. THE DECISION TO BE LOOKED AT.** Sixteen days since she wrote five and the room has made no sound about it, and she stops going under the table on the fourth day because she works out that going under it is a thing she does because she has nothing else to do with an hour and that is not the same as looking for something. **She says out loud that the only way a room finds out anything is by being given something to look at, and that a strip of pale wood put on that table by the person it is about is a thing that arrives.** She says the part she does not want to say: **there is one look and there is no second, and if the look is what puts the five in a row then the five stops being a thing she did, and she has been escaping being entered since she was nine years old — and then she says she is going to put it on that table anyway.** She goes down to the fourth landing and finds **three marks cut into the flat of that rail, one for each time a boy of sixteen came up that tower, and no mark for going down.** She goes back up with nothing in her hands and puts the strip on the top of that table at the near end for the first time in a hundred and eighty days.
- **ch 767, days 2,485–2,487, the twelfth to the fourteenth, *What One Look Is Worth*. THE COST, PUT INTO WORDS BEFORE THE ACT.** The deciding takes one look and there is no second look and there has never been anything to appeal with, and she says out loud that **she has wanted one since she came up that stair and has never once said so out loud in nineteen years.** She spends a day on the difference between a thing put in front of a hand and a thing given to a person, and gets the answer she does not like: **in a hundred and eighty-five days nothing has been given to her, and putting a piece of wood down on a table and having a hand read it is not being given a thing, and it is worse, because a thing that is put in front of a hand comes back having been read.** She goes down and stands on the fourth landing and says out loud that there is not one thing she could hand him that would let him measure anything. And then she says the decision out loud **before** she does it, because the fourth of the six terms is that she says every line before it is a line anywhere else, **and she gets a board out of the stack, because a thing that arrives has to arrive and not be laid there by her own hand.**
- **ch 768, days 2,487–2,489, the fourteenth to the sixteenth, *Thirty Feet From A Copying Floor*. THE DEFINITION, AND THE REASON SHE IS NOT GOING DOWN.* She stands in front of the locked drawer thirty feet from the copying floor, measures it, puts her fingers on the grey in the wood where a hand goes, and does not open it, and says out loud that the reason she gave on the nineteenth of the month beginning 2,444 still holds. Then she says **what a row is**, for the first time in this building, and it comes out in four pieces in a count of ninety — a place a thing is decided about · a head that is a figure and a figure is what the thing costs · a hand that fills it in and writes what a person said · and a thing that arrives with no row is administered and waits — **and then she stops, because there is a fifth piece she has never worked out, and the fifth piece is the column, and she has been saying row and meaning the head.** She works out how to get twenty-two paces of a four-foot table to a shelf with a board in her hand and says out loud that **she has been making twenty-two paces into eight hundred and eighty steps because it is easier to be frightened of a bigger number**, and goes down and puts her hand into the cold iron of the cage that has been out since the third of the month beginning 2,294.
- **ch 769, days 2,490–2,492, the seventeenth to the nineteenth, *The First Look*. THE LOOK, AND WHAT THE ROOM WROTE.** She gets a board out of the stack backwards on her back with a cross-beam across her shoulders, goes up the table twenty-two paces, kneels on the shelf in front of that lamp, and says the whole of that row out loud **because the hand writes what a person said and nothing else** — the head, ten days, twenty-one hours and thirty-six minutes; the same figure in the first pair of cuts; **and five, in the second pair, in her own hand, written on the twenty-second of the month beginning 2,444 at the ninth hour with a nib and a cake of ink against her fourth rib.** The hands come off the near edge and the head comes down over the wood the way it comes down over forty boards on the first of a month, and it stays there for about eleven seconds, **and she does not move, and she works out on her knees afterwards that she can count eleven without her breath going off nine on the fourth count, and that the answer to what she has been failing at since the fifth of the month beginning 2,444 is that something had its attention on the shelf she was kneeling on.** Then a board comes out of the forty and is put down in front of her, **with a stroke and a figure on it: her price again, at the head and in the column, and not one stroke in the second pair of cuts.** She ends on the two boards in that stack carrying the same figure and on the question she cannot answer: **a room does not report what it looked at, a room reports what it wrote, and what it wrote is a price.**
- **ch 770, days 2,492–2,496, the nineteenth to the twenty-third, *The Second Pair Of Cuts*. WHAT A ROOM DOES WITH A COLUMN IT CANNOT ADD UP.** She leaves the board on the table for four days and the room looks at it once more and writes nothing, and she says the plainest thing she has said in that room in a hundred and ninety-two days: **she gave a room the first look it has ever had at anything of hers and it wrote down what she cost and it did not touch the five, and that is not a room that is going to do anything else.** She goes down four hundred and forty and says the whole of the coming month out loud on a landing where there is nothing that copies figures exactly. And then she works out the answer to her own question: **a room writes a figure once and the figure is what the thing costs; a room writes a figure twice and it has made a decision twice, and a decision made twice is one thing, and what it has decided is that the figure is right.** She says it plainly and then sits down against a leg of that table and says what she was feeling, **which is that she wanted to be looked at and that what she got was standing in the way of a look, and that it is the best thing that has happened to her in this building and it is not going to be pretended otherwise, and what it did was confirm that she is a price.**
- **ch 771, days 2,497–2,500, the twenty-fourth to the twenty-seventh, *Two Boards With One Figure*. THE FAILURE, AND THE ANGER.** She goes under the table and turns the forty boards over and finds the one she wrote five on and puts a different one face down under the near end, **and it does not matter.** She works out the reason on the twenty-fifth and it is not a rule in this building: **a thing that has not been read is a thing that has not arrived, and a thing that has been read is a thing that has arrived, and there is no procedure in this county for a thing that has arrived and does not go where arrived things go, and there has never been one because not one person in four hundred years has ever wanted one to go somewhere else.** She goes over the four ways she tried it on her own in one night and does not say the fifth, which is the one she agreed not to use. She goes down and finds **a fourth mark on the flat of that rail that is not that boy's** and cannot say when it was cut and does not guess. And on the twenty-seventh she does the sums and says the last of it **angry, the only time in a hundred and ninety-nine days that she has said anything in that room in any other voice**: two boards with her price on them are going up that stair in four days and she has had two days to stop it and **it is not that anybody has refused her anything, because there has not been one thing for her to ask for until the sixth of this month, and what she said then was that she is not going back to a brother two days of road and six hundred feet below her.**
- **ch 772, days 2,504 and 2,505, the first and the second, *What A Room Can Do Once*. MIDPOINT — THE ROOM OFFERS TO AMEND THE ROW ONCE, AND EVERY PART OF IT IS TRUE.** Forty boards come up the turn in an armful at the second hour and go along that table in ten minutes at about four to a minute, **and she says nothing while it goes, because she said on the twenty-seventh that she would not.** Two of them carry her price and she cannot tell which. At the fourth hour the light comes down to the cut in the wall, the fourth first of a month the mark has fallen on. **At the second hour of the second of that month a hand goes into that cage and the light comes up past the top of it and the forearm at that table goes down by the width of the little finger on her left hand — and then the one at that table speaks, for the first time in the volume, and does not turn round and does not look up and does not stop working.** It says, in about eleven seconds: there is a column in this county adding one day in twenty since the ninth hour of the twenty-fourth of the month beginning 2,054 with a figure at the head of it that has never been in a row above this line · **it can amend one row and has never amended one, and that is what the amending is for** · it will put that figure into hers, in the pair of cuts under the one she wrote, over what she wrote there, **and then her row will add up** · **it costs one feed** · the feed for that lamp is paid by that hand out of itself once every twenty-nine days and an amending is not that, **and the feed would therefore be hers** · it has to be written in the light, **and the light comes down to that cut at the fourth hour of the first of the month beginning 2,534, and if a hand is not in that cage before it gets there it does not come up again** · **and it is not going to put a day in there on its own and have nothing for it, which has not happened here in four hundred years** · and then the one thing nobody in that room has ever said to her: **if a name goes in that column then the figure she wrote stops being hers and becomes the column's, and a column is not a person, and you can argue with a person.** She says one word back, standing, in daylight, in the ordinary voice: **two figures in one column is a column that gets counted, and the room knows that, and the room is being kind to me by telling me so, and there is not one person in this building who has ever been kind to me before.** And she has not said yes and she has not said no.
- **ch 773, day 2,509, the sixth, *What She Has Got*. THE COUNT.** She counts what she has, standing, in daylight, out loud, in a room with two people in it: **four fingers and a thumb · two coppers she cannot count · five blank leaves against her fourth rib that she has not written in and is not going to · a record kept out loud with six terms and no seventh · nine hundred and fifty days by `D − 1,559`, one of them gone into a lamp.** She says the six terms and finds that **the record is the only one of the five that somebody else put there, and the only one that would cost somebody else something if she lost it.** She says the arithmetic out loud as arithmetic and not as anything else. **And she says the sixth thing and does not count it: there is a bar nine feet nine against the wall on the left of that room and she is not going to count it as something she has, not because it is not hers but because she put it down on purpose, and the putting down is the only thing about that bar that is hers and everything else about it belongs to a man who got it when she was nine.** She ends on the finding: **four of the five are dead weight and the fifth is the only thing in the world that can be spent** — and she is not going to decide on the strength of a purse. **She is going to decide it on the sixth term, and she has not said the sixth term out loud in that room since the twenty-eighth of the month beginning 2,384.**
- **ch 774, day 2,513, the tenth, *What The Figure Is For*. THE ACCOUNTING, IN HER OWN MOUTH, ON HER OWN FIGURE.** Nine hundred and fifty-four days. She does the arithmetic of the offer — **the amendment would go in the second pair of cuts down in that column over the five, at the second hour of the second of the month beginning 2,534, and between the fourth hour of that first and the second hour of that second there is a window of about ten hours in which that room would have light in it and no decision in it, and that is the whole of the time there is.** Then she asks of each of the five what it is for, one at a time, out loud, standing, in daylight, as she has done since she was nine. The hand writes. The coppers are for nothing, flatly, in ninety counts. The five leaves are for a thing she has not got to yet. The six terms are so that there is one thing in her life that she says out loud before it happens — **and she did not know until this morning that keeping them in a room with no daylight in it was a difference.** And the last one: **nine hundred and fifty-four days. What is that for. It is for light.** And then the sentence the whole day came to: **a figure that goes down is the only thing in the world that can pay for light, and a room is a place where a figure goes down, and she worked that out on a barrow ground when she was nine years old with a bar in her hands and did not know then that it was a working out.** She has been in rooms for nineteen years and none of them had one, and she could not be given a thing and could not be measured and could not be counted where she stood, **and she did not know any of that was because of the figure.**
- **ch 775, days 2,514–2,523, the eleventh to the twentieth, *The Sixty-Third Day*. THE SIX TERMS SAID OUT LOUD AGAIN, AND THE STANDING STILL, AND WHY SIXTY-THREE.** She says all six out loud in that room, one at a time, standing up, in daylight, in the ordinary voice, **for the first time in two hundred and thirteen days** — and finds that the only one she has not been keeping is the sixth, which is that she decides everything, and that **what she has been doing for two hundred and thirteen days without saying it is deciding.** On the eighteenth she works out why she is going to refuse, and it is the fourth term: **every line has to be said out loud as she goes before it is a line anywhere else, and the amendment would put a figure in a column that nobody in that building has ever said out loud, and the room has never amended a row and there is no instrument in the world that amends a row twice, so she would never be able to check it once.** Then on the nineteenth, the twentieth and the twenty-first she stands in the middle of the floor of that room, which is the middle of nothing, **and gets a count of eleven out of it four times in a row, and the reason is not that she has got better at it: being looked at is what holds a person still and there is not one thing in that room looking at her, and it turns out that is not the only way.** And on the twenty-first she says the interval out loud as arithmetic and not as a lesson and has no rule for it, **and what she has instead of a rule is that she can hold still on a stone floor and she could not do it on the twentieth of that month and could not do it when she took the last one**, and she says she is going to take the sixth stage off that table in the morning.
- **ch 776, day 2,524, the twenty-first, *The Second Look*. CLIMAX — IMMORTAL VESSEL 6, THE SECOND LOOK, AND THE COST NAMED ONCE.** She takes a third board out of the stack herself, goes up the table, kneels on the shelf, **says the stage out loud in the ordinary voice standing up in her own head, and writes six** with the nib against her fourth rib, three fingers and a thumb, on an open arm — **and her right forearm goes down by the width of the little finger on her left hand while the nib is still on the wood.** The interval is said out loud: sixty-three days, and she has not worked out a rule for fifty-eight or for a hundred and thirteen and is not going to work one out for this. **And then the person at that table looks up.** They had not looked up once in the whole of the volume before it — not when a hand came off the near edge on the eighteenth of the month beginning 2,444, not when she wrote a five on the twenty-second, not on the seventeenth of this month. **It took eleven seconds and there was nothing she could do with her face.** Then they went back to the near edge and put their hands on the wood, **and nothing was written.** No board out of the stack, no stroke, no figure. **She says out loud that they looked at her and did not write anything down, and that a room that looks at a thing once decides what it is, and that they have already spent the one look on a foot of pale wood on the seventeenth, and there is no second look, and they looked at her anyway.** And then the cost, named once: **Immortal Vessel 6, six stages into the seventh realm, at sixty-three days after the fifth and a hundred and twenty-one days after the fourth, and it has cost me the ability to be looked at again.** She has wanted to be looked at since the seventh of the month beginning 2,474. She has had two looks in five days and one of them was at a foot of pale wood and one of them was at her, **and it is the last one there is going to be, and she is nineteen years old and she has got a thing she wanted and has not got a use for it.** She puts the board with a six on it back under the shelf in the stack of forty and cannot tell which of the two it was when she had put it down.
- **ch 777, days 2,525–2,531, the twenty-second to the twenty-eighth, *A Word She Has Not Heard*. THE REFUSAL, AND IT IS NOT PRIDE.** She takes four days to work out what refusing costs, and what it costs is the second of the six terms: **there is no daylight in that room, there is a lamp in a cage at the far end of a table and the wall behind the light is the wall and there is not one window in this building she has been able to see from the inside, and she has been saying that term out loud in lamplight for two hundred and twenty-six days and it is the only one of the six she has been getting wrong, and if that lamp goes out she cannot say it again in this room and not in any room, because there is not one other room she has been in since she was nine that has daylight in it either.** On the twenty-sixth she does the last of the working out: **there has not been one figure in that building for two hundred and twenty-eight days that got into a row that she had not said out loud there first, and the room has never amended a row, and there is no instrument in the world that amends a row twice, and so she would never be able to check it. Not once. Not ever.** And at the ninth hour of the twenty-seventh she says it out loud, standing, in daylight, in the ordinary voice, in front of the room: **I am not going to put a day into that lamp** — and then the rest of it, **and she says out loud that she is not refusing it because she is proud and she wants that said out loud in this room because there is not one person in that building who is going to say it for her**, and that the figure the amending would put in that column is the room's and she has not seen it, **and the figure she wants standing is hers, a five, in her own hand, wrong the moment after it is written**, and she would rather have a wrong figure in a column that cannot be added up than a right figure in one that can. Then she says the six terms out loud every day for three days on purpose, and says out loud that the thirtieth will be the last time she says the second one in that room, **and says out loud that on the seventh of the month beginning 2,534 there is going to be no light in that room and a boy of sixteen is going to come up it anyway because he cannot leave a thing alone.**
- **ch 778, days 2,532–2,534, the twenty-ninth to the first, *Down The Way It Came*. IRREVERSIBLE — THE LAMP GOES OUT, AND A BUNDLE GOES BACK DOWN THE STAIR.** On the twenty-ninth she says all six out loud one last time in daylight and **says the second one and stops for ninety counts and then says it anyway**, because the fourth term is that she says it before it is a line anywhere else, and she says: **that was the last time.** On the thirtieth she does the last day of it the way she does a day, takes a reading of her forearm, does the sums, and says the last of it **to the room and not to the person at it**: **there is one thing left that can be put in that lamp and it is nine hundred and seventy-four days of me, and the person at this table has told me the true shape of it and they were right about every part of it, and so it is me or nothing, and I am not going to.** Then, at the second hour of the first of the month beginning 2,534, **the man comes up the turn with forty boards and a bundle of papers and puts them down, and the hands at the near edge of that table do not come off the wood, and forty things have arrived and the room has not looked at one of them.** She says *Take them back down* and he does not ask why, **and she says out loud that it is the first time in four hundred years that anybody has said that sentence in this room, and that she is not going to untie the bundle.** He goes back down eight hundred and eighty steps with everything he carried up. And at the fourth hour **the light came down the last two of her own hand to the cut, and it did not stop on the cut, and it went under it, and it went out.** And she says out loud why: **it has stopped on that cut four hundred years, not because the cut stops it, but because a hand has gone into that cage within a day of the light reaching that cut every single time, and the one thing that cut has ever been is the place they stand and watch from — and no hand went into that cage.** And there is nothing in the world that can put it back. **And in the dark she says: the bar is still there and I did not move it · there is a row in this room that says what I am, written by me, true, and there is no light in it · the deciding takes one look and there has never been a need to appeal, because this room has never had to decide anything in the dark · and it has not decided anything about me · and I did that.**
- **ch 779, days 2,535–2,537, the second to the fourth, *Nine Hundred And Four In The Dark*. THE PHYSICAL CARD, AND THE SETTLING.** She stands in the dark for ninety counts and says two sentences: **the light came down to that cut at the fourth hour of the first and went under it and it has not come back, and I did that.** Then she finds **the bundle of papers under the far end of that table is against the third leg from the near end and it was against the second**, and she says one sentence about it on the flat of her back on the stone: **something in this building has been paid for and I did not feel it go** — and then that she has no instrument, cannot tell what it was or whose it was or what it was worth, **and that she has said out loud in that building before today that she is the only thing in it that can pay for anything, and that is not true and she does not know when it stopped being true.** Then she goes down the second stair at the second hour of the sixth **with nothing in either hand**, says the numbers out loud one at a time the way she has said every number she has ever been afraid of losing, with the wall at every hundred and the wall four inches off, **and comes up all of it, and counts it coming up, and it is nine hundred and four, and it is not the same stair as the one in the tower and the two of them are never added anywhere and have never been added anywhere in four hundred years.** And on the seventh she goes down the first four hundred and forty with her hand on the rail, does not go down the second four, puts her hand into the cage on that landing and the iron is cold, stands in front of the locked drawer and does not open it, and says out loud: **there is a lamp in a cage on this landing that has been out for two hundred and forty-three days and there is a lamp in a cage at the top of that tower that has been out for two days, and I put one of them out, and there is a boy of sixteen who keeps one of these going and there is no oil in this building and there has not been for nine years.** She comes back up into the dark and cannot see her own hand on the near edge of that table, and says: **there is a strip of pale wood under this table with a price at the head of it and a five in the column in my own hand and twelve pairs of cuts under that are empty, and I know every one of those cuts by where it is under my fingers, and that is not a thing anybody did to me. I did that.**
- **ch 780, days 2,538–2,540, the fifth to the seventh, *Eight Hundred And Eighty Steps*. THE CLOSE.** Three days in the dark and she goes down neither stair. She says the six terms out loud every day and **says the second one last of all and says it anyway.** And on the fifth she **says a name out loud in the middle of them, once, because a record has to say what it is between** — and nothing in that room made a sound, and **there is not one thing in that room she can tell has heard anything, and she is not going down two days of road to find out what was written.** And she works out that she has an arithmetic and it is not one sentence and **she has never once in nineteen years had a person who wanted one sentence.** On the sixth she is on the fourth landing with her hand in the cold cage and **hears the tower's own stair and it takes a long time and it stops four times**, and she says *That is him*, and she goes back up the four hundred and forty without running, faster than she has gone up that stair in the whole of the volume, and stands four feet off the near end of that table in the dark and listens to him come the whole way, **and there was not one thing in that room to see him by.** At the seventh hour of the seventh he comes into that room with an oil lamp in his hand, **the first light in that room in six days, and it puts a hand's width of itself along the near end of that table and goes no further, and the twenty-two paces from there to the far end stay exactly as they had been.** He looks at her and nothing in that room decides anything when he does it, **and it is the first time since the ninth hour of the twenty-first of the month beginning 2,504 that anything had looked at her.** He asks why it was lit and she gives him the whole arithmetic — the cage, the fifty-eight of her own hand, two a day, twenty-nine days, a hand in that cage at the second hour of the second of every month, eight times watched, **it came down to that cut at the fourth hour of the first of the month beginning 2,534 and it did not stop on it, and that is because there was no hand in it, and there was no hand in it because I did not put one in — and I did that on purpose and I have not got a reason you would want.** He says *That is not why* and she says *I know that is not why*. **Then he walks about the table and finds the second place and says that is worn by something being put in the same place a great many times over a very long time and it is not a hand**, and she says out loud, for the first time in the volume, that it is not a chair, that it is addressed, that it is the right size for somebody who is not here, that not one instruction in that room put anybody in it, and that she cannot tell him what it is for. **And then she says the whole of what is in that room, out loud, standing, in the dark** — the bar, the nib and the cake of ink against her fourth rib, the bundle corded round twice and not knotted, the strip with ten days, twenty-one hours and thirty-six minutes at the head and the same figure in the first pair of cuts and five in the second and twelve pairs empty, and no light in the room and not one instrument in the world that could read one word of it — and then the figures: **nine hundred and eighty-one days, at one hour and twelve minutes a day, and not one of them going back and one of them gone into that lamp and one of them that light · two hundred and forty-six days that this arm has been open, and three fingers and a thumb on the hand that writes with, and nine hundred and four steps at the foot of that room and eight hundred and eighty at the top of it, and a boy of sixteen in a doorway and a brother two days of road and six hundred feet below me and a voice in my head that is a person and that I am not speaking to · and a lamp that has been out for six days that I put out.** And then he says *that is a lot* and *I did not come up here for that*, and asks **is there anybody in here**, and she tells him about the one at that table who has stood at it for four hundred years and **does not know whether anything in there can tell the difference between a room with a person in it and a room with a lamp burning in it.** And the last line of Volume 13: **she had a sentence for him and has not got it, and she does not have an answer that is small.**

**What changed that could not have changed before it:** **she spent the one look there is on a foot of pale wood and the room wrote her price again and she cannot tell whether it saw the five** · **a room that writes a figure twice has decided it twice** · **the one at that table spoke once in sixty chapters and offered to amend her row once, and every part of it was true, and it was the only kind thing that room has ever done to her** · **she said out loud what a row is and found the fifth piece of it she had never worked out, which is the column** · **four of the five things she has are dead weight and the fifth is the only thing in the world that can be spent, and it is for light, and she did not put it there** · **the second of the six terms is in daylight and there is no daylight in that room** · **she can hold still on a stone floor because she has something to be still for** · **she is six stages into the seventh realm and cannot be looked at again, and she wanted it** · **the one at that table looked at her for eleven seconds and wrote nothing** · **she refused the amendment because she could never check the line** · **the lamp went out, and it did not stop on the cut, and there is not one thing in that building that goes into a lamp in a cage** · **a bundle went back down that stair the way it came for the first time in four hundred years** · **merit settled once in the volume, on the page, off the ground that room stands on, with no figure of it** · **nine hundred and four steps in the dark with nothing in her hands, down and up, and the two stairs were never added** · **a boy of sixteen stood in a doorway with an oil lamp, asked why, was told an arithmetic, noticed the second place at that table, and did not get what he came up eight hundred and eighty steps for** · **and the last line of the volume is her not having an answer that is small.**
